SELECTED READINGS.

FAIILT-FINDING.—AS It woman, the fault-finder, if less formidable because with less power than when a man, is infinitely annoying. She leaves no one in peace. The terror of her family, she is their unauthorised putter to rights, and down to her

grand-nieces themselves she does her best to obstruct their lives and model them on her own. A terror to her family and a bore to her friends, the fauit-finder is the despair of her servants. No one can pease her. Faithful maid and careful man, it is all the same as if the one were good- for-nothing, and the other a rogue. However well done a thing may be, it could always have been better, and the fault-finder takes special care not to spoil her domestic with an overdose of praise. She never praises, indeed, if she can by any possibility blame. When the very decencies of life oblige her to give some kind of commenda- tion, she gives it so sparingly as to deprive it of all beauty ; or she modifies it with streaks of blame so thickly strewn as to spoil all its grace. If her ser- vants serve her well, it is because she herself knows hose to make bad material into good. When they serve her ill, she has not herself to blame, bat only their wickedness. So she goes on, this poor ill-catured, cross-cornered cr.mture, find- ing fault where a more amiable soul would see only cause for praise; thankless when others would be grateful; unloving wizen others would be devoted; a dry, sour winter apple which never reddens into beanty :nor ripens into sweetness. These fault- finders drift through their world like so many humanisedticebergs, in the presence of which all warmth of lite ad spontaneity die, while only sharp- ness and coldness and angularity recsain.—E. Lynn Linton. Thal ENGLISH AND '2IIR FRENCH BOIL—The French boy constitutes a striking contrast to his English brother. In every way there is a difference. Not only at the outset, but up to the time of his quitting school, a boy in France is tre-ited as a machine which needs constaut supervision and restriction, and which, in default of such unceasing care, is sure to go wrong. The lad is taught to do eyerything )sy fixed rule and at reolar hours, to rely in all things upon the initiative of others. All individuality, all idea of personal respousibdity, is thus destroyed. Perhaps it would be more correct to say that it is never suffered to develop itself. The playground of a French school would be a revelation to the average English boy. He would in his own mind specelate vaguely as to the purpose for which it was oesigned. It is too small tor play as he understands the word, and, as a matter of fact, only the very young boys play. The elder lads confine themselves to a monotonous peregrin- ation of what is often a mere courtyard. It is true that the French schoolboy is taught gymnastics far more systematically than is always the case in this country. But there his physical education has begun and ended, and even this modified form of development of the body has not invariably been without its opponents. It is not, after all, so many years ago since the Minister of Public Instruction addressed a circular to the rectors of the principal educational establishments in France pointing out that complaints had been made regarding the number of accidents which occulted in the practice of gymnastics. The matter was referred to a com- mittee, and it was only after some discussion that It was decided that the advantages, on the whole, outweighed the chances of disaster. WEALTEI AND HEALTH.—That wealth is any enemy to health seems a contradictory statement at first sight, but, tniedy considered, it shows itself unfortunately to he anything but that. The making of money keeps a man occupied and healthy, bat it Is found when the fortune is accumulated that the mischief begins. The worn-out old rhyme that "Early to bed and early to rise, keeps a man healthy, wealthy, and wise" has a great deal of truth in it, for we naturally conclude that the man who takes the trouble to "rise" early, would scarcely do so unless he meant to fill up the time thus gained by giving exercise to his physical and mental powers, and to this species c,f individual wealth does no harm. But when, having Strived and accumulated, a man sits down to take life easily, whlch means very often luxury, self-indul- gence, and dtmieished activity of mind and body, wealth is distitmly an enemy to long life and health, not only to the one gerleration, but to those who follow after, and who suffer from the sins of their fathers unto the third and fourth generation. —The Hospital. FRANCIS BACON (afterwards Lord Bacon) was born in the strand, in 1651. His health was delicate; and by his gravity of carriage, and love of sedentary pursuits, he was distinguished from other boys. When a mere child, he stole away from his playfellows to a vault in St. James's Fields to investigate the cause of a singular echo which he had observed there; and when only 12, he busied himself with speculations in the art of leger- demain. At 13 he was entered at Trinity College, Cambridge, which he left after a residence of three years, "carrying with him a profound contempt for the course of study pursued there, a fixed conviction that the system of academic education in England was radically vicious, a just scorn to; the trifles on which the followers of Aristotle had wasted their powers, and no great revnence for Aristotle him- self."—From Timb's School Day's of Eminent Men..

Toe younger a child is, the less let him bear the Unspeakable named, but let him behold His sym- bols. The sublime is the temple-step of religion, ES the stars are of immeasurabie space. When what is mighty appears in nature—a storm, thunder, the starry firmament, death—then utter the word God before the child. A great misfortune, a great blessing, a noble action, are building stones for the boy's church. Show everywhere to the child, as well as on the borders of the holy land of religion, devotional and holy sentiments. Newton, who uncovered his head when the Greatest Name was uttered, would have been, without saying a word, a teacher of religion to children—Jean Paul Richter. Toe productive classes of the world include those who bless it by th. ir thought as well as those who bless it by their work. He who invents a • machine does no less a service than he who toils all day with his hands. Thus, the inventors of the plot 0), the loom, and the ship bate been deservedly , placed among those whom society ought to honour. Bet they also who teach men moral and religious truth—who instruct them to think, to live together in peace, to loc.; one another, and to pass good lives—are benefactors of the world, nay,' its greatest benefactors. They speak to the deepest wants of the soul, and eiv- men the true bread from heaven. —Theodore Parker. THE worst condition the heart of a man can be in Is to be empty. It is a common mistal eto suppose that the needs and the craviegs of the heart can be wholly supplied to it from outside sources, that all It wants is the love and sympathy, the kindness and tenderness of others poured upon it. A much deeper need of the heart is to he full of affection and goodwill and genercus irnpulees of its own, to be able to sympathise in other,' joys and sorrows, to have sech an abundance of rich heart-treasures to bestow that there is but little thought of what it is to receive. To ridicule the oddities of our neighbours it wit of the cheapest and easiest kind; and we can all.be satirical if we give the mime to our ill-nature. The jest so ariwircr to curselves may, however, Inflict a deep wound upon a eeasitive nature, while it seldom fells to brieg a heavy retribution upon the author. EVERYWHERE IA the natural, no less than in the moral wood, light is the great lite-bringer. With- out it there is no e.ermaneet and deep,leing beauty. Well may all natiems, ih all agss; )istva called wisdom by the muce of darkness, and 'transferred the names of light and i,riehtte-ss to whatever is happy and holy.—Lee ii. "CAPITAL" CHAT. Dr. Samuel Cox, who died at Hastings on Monday was one of the leading ministers of the Baptist denomination, lie first became prominent about thirty years ago, when his " Private letters of St. Paul and St. John" were welcomed by the Press as a revelation in Biblical criticism. Dr. Cox has written much since then, but probably his reputa- tion will chiefly rest upon his " Salvator Mandl," in which the popular notion of an eternal chemical hell is subjected to the test of reason as well as scholar- ship. The "Expositorrin connection with which Dr. Cox's name is perhaps most widely known, was started eighteen years ago. A staff of brilliant scholars contributed to it, including Canon Farrar, the Bishop of Durham, and Dr. Marcus Dodds. Ulti- mately Dr. Cox became too broad for his chair, and the magazine of his creation fell into othei hands. The Bishop-elect of Norwich will rather striking contrast to his predecessor in the See. Dr. Pelham is a placid Evangelical who has adminis- tered his diocese on old-fashioned lines, and has not outrun his clergy in energy; Mr. Sheepshanks is a broad-minded High Churchman, who serving his apprenticeship to parochial work under Dr. Hook at Leeds, and with a varied experience home and colonial, is imbued with the new methods that have so greatly revived the influence of the Church in our large centres of pop ilation. He is emphati- cally a worker, 'end whatever he has taken in hand he has thrown his whole soul into the undertaking. He is a brilliant conversationalist, and his range of interest and of reading is almost as wide as the Premier's, to whom he owes his mitre. ia at

Ma special meeting of the Institute of British Architects on Monday night, Mr. J. Macvicar Anderson was unanimously nominated president .for the ensuing year. Mr. Anderson is already in his second year of office, and this nomination con- sequently involved the suspension of the bye-law to the effect that no president shall fill the office for more than two successive years. Mr. Anderson is a Scotch man of dignified and aristocratic appear- ance, precise in the matter of dress and in his man- ner of procedure. He makes an excellent chairman, being always clear in his ruling and courteous. 114 has always been regular in the discharge of his official duties, and being one who has the interests of the institute at heart, members should congratu- late themselves on being fortunate to retain his services for another year.

The members of the Cage Bird Club established a few days ago will be able to quote many royal examples in defence of their hobby. The Princess Beatrice's favorite cage-bird is the canary, and for many years the same might have been said of the Queen. When Ilendelssohn was playing at Court on one occasion her Majesty's birds were turned unceremoniously out of the music-room by their mistress's orders, because their powerful vocalisa- tion disturbed the serenity of the gifted composer, who used to say that her Majesty knew his com- positions more thoroughly than anyone else in England. In recent years the Q leen has found the singing of the yellow-coated birds too strenuous, and at present her canaries have given place to a bullfinch and a linnet. These follow her every- where, there being special provision for their com- fort is the royal train, just as there is for the three pet dogs.

General Patrick Collins. who is to succeed Mr. New as Consul-General for the United States in London, will hold the most lucrative position in the gift of the American President. Soon after his election last November, Mr. Clevelanni offered General Collins, the Secretaryship of the Navy, or the Attorney-Generalship. He declined each, on the ground that he could not afford to maintain such an establishment as would be required of him in Washington, on the comparatively small salary he would draw from the Government. But he promptly accepted the lucrative London post. General Collins was born near Fermoy, in county Cork, Ireland, in 1844. When he was four years old his parents took him to the States. At the upholsterer's trade he earned sufficient funds to enter Harvard College. He was admitted to prac- tise at the Boston Bar when twenty-four years of age. His wife was Miss Mary Carey: They had three children. The family mansion on the summit of Mount Ida commands a fine view of Boston Harbour. General Collins is one of the two or three most distinguished Irishmen in America.

Both Lord Salisbury and his i-srivate secretary, Mr. Gunton, have now happily reco%sred from their attack of influenza. Mr. Gunton is a stalwart man in the prime ot life, who looks very much like a Hertfordshire farmer. His quarters at Hatfield are very comfortable; in fact there are several cottages within hail of Hatfield House in the occupation of Lord Salisbury's agent and others which, for solid roominess and cosy elegance surpass the provision made at Windsor for the servants holding similar offices in the royal household. It goes without say- tog that electrical communications and other scientific appliances are developed to an unusual extent at Hatfield. During the last seven years it has been necessary for the ex-Foreign Secretary to leave his private affairs a good deal in the hands of Mr. Gunton, who, in fact, transacts a vast amount of local business without consaltincr his chief at all. Amongst other duties of this kind' is the granting of permission to school excursions to make Hatfield their headquarters. Lady Salisbury frequently gives instructions, having in view the greater com- fort of these tiny trippers, especially in wet wea- ther.

The fineness of the weather brought out all the fashionable people who are left in London in the Park, on Sunday morning, but the talk was largely of going away. The latest fashion in capes appears to be the "Harlequin "—that is to say, half of one colour and half of another, alternating. An object of attraction was an American trotting buggy, painted scarlet, with two fine steppers attached, which ran up and down with great speed. Among the many handsome ladies of all ages who were visible were Lady Carew, Lady Morris and her daughter, Lady Cotterell and Miss Louise Cotterell, Lady Carrington, Lady Camoys, Lady Robert Mon- tagu, Lady Ward, Lady Randolph Churchill, Lady Cairns. Lady Medway, the Hon. Mrs. Hylton Joliffe, Lady Seymour, Lady McGrigor, Mrs. Richard Cham- berlain, Lady Stewart, the Hon. Mrs. Randolph Stewart. Among the male persqpalities who passed about were Mr. Alfred de Rothschild, Lord Morris, Lord Camden, the Hon. Gerald McGarel Hogg, Lord

Newtown Butler, Lord Arthur Hill, Lord W Olmer, Mr. George Alexander, Mr. W. H. Mallock, Sir Eyre Shaw, the Hon. Kenneth Howard, and Mr. Martin Morris.

Sir George Findlay was the doyen of general rail- way managers. !le spent his business life in an atmosphere of speaking tubes and contracts, abstruse calculations and puzzling statistics; but even the great weight of business that presses on the manager of such a gigantic concern as the Lon- don and North-Western Railway had made few in- roads on a portly frame and magnificent constitu- tion up to a short time before his death. The reason was that Sir George Findlay never took business home with him. While at Euston there was no one so alert, far seeing, and quick to decide as he; but elsewhere he was the country gentle- man, with a passionate love of field sports. He was a good shot and an ardent salmon fisher, and, in the rusticity of his old-fashioned house at Edg- ware, took delight to show his pictures, and a gar- den stocked with the flowers that were the delight of our forefathers.

The late manager joined the London and North Western Railway Company when they bought the Shrewsbury and Hereford line in 1862. He was, he used to say, "taken over with the rest of the stock." He came to Euston in 1865, and nine years later was appointed general manager. He was a master of his business. His fellow managers recognied this,when they chose him first chairman Of the Railway Clearing House General Managers' Association. Sir George looked like a farmer, with his full, clean-shaven face, comfortable waist- ccat, and grey mutton-chop whiskers, straggling down the sides of his well-formed but sparsley covered head.

According to the official returns published by the Ministry of Communication,l2,ooo persons have suffered from railway accidents in Russia during the past ten years. Of this number 4,500 were killed and 7.500 wounded. Most of these accidents are attributed to the imperfect methods of railway signalling in vogue in the ountry.

WALKER, BART. A BREWER'S ENORMOUS FORTUNE.

I

Present visitors to Paris come back laden with their spoils in black satin, for nothing else is being worn there just now. The hats are plainly covered with the inevitable satin which is most becoming to the complexion; and it is, after all, much too early as yet for straws, excepting in the shop win. dows. Four black tips are arranged in the fr6nt ; beneath these, a little to one side, is a paste buckle, which holds in its place a band of eveque bishop's purple violet, which at the back forms a gr6cefully careless bow. A new feature in dress is the skirt with many vertical rows of trimming. This innovation will entail more trouble in dressmaking. The formality of the horizontal lines is broken by the fulness of the skirt, which gradnally widens from the waist downwards until at the hem it is full. To produce this effect every breadth of the skirt is sloped a little at each side. If the material be narrow, like silk, this is simple. If double width, like cloth, the goring begins at the sides, both back and side breadths being cut away, so that two half-bias are joined together. Black skirts can be wonder- fully improved by three or tour narrowish bands of bright green or petunia-coloured velvet. The out- door black jacket should be made to match by having big sleeves of velvet put in.

A favourite,new method of trimming hats and bonnets appears rather curious at first, but, like all really innocent innovations in style, it gains in favour with closer acqua'idance. There are ears, sometimes of pleated lisse, sometimes of velvet, occasionally of ostrich fer-ther, and again of jet. In some iestances they are crescent-shaped, and are often fashioned like those of a Mercury's cap. Many of the bonnets, trimmed with fans of jewel- flecked tulle, have one diamond ornament pinned into the fltt point which nestles down in the hair in fr ,nt. The favourite shape of this brooch, which may be of any jewels, is rosette form.

Various Kinds of Broth.

./1 it 'IV •

There are many simple drinks for invalids which are nourishing and soothing and which have not become hackneyed to the taste,as beef tea and some others have. One very nice drink is known in France as " lait de poule." To make this, beat up the yoke of a perfectly fresh egg with a teaspoonful of powdered sugar. Add to it very slowly, beating all the time, a cup of boiling milk. Finally stir in a tablespoonful of fresh cream, and serve the beverage at once. Where a purely stimulating drink is required there is probably nothing better than beef tea, though it dce s not possess the quality of nour- ishment so often attributed to it. Milk, however, eon. tains nutriment on which a person may live in health, and a drink of hot milk and eggs is especially valuable where a nutritious beverage is required. When an egg is beaten up in a cup of beef tea, a considerable nutri- ment is imparted and &valuable and stimulating beverage is the result. Calf's foot broth is an agreeable drink,but it is nutritious only because milk and eggs are added to it. To make calf's foot broth, boil slowly two calves' feet, putting them in cold water and letting them sim- mer for about ten hours. There will be about a pint and a half of stock, or liquor, around them when they are done. Strain this and set it away in a cold place till it jellies. If it is to be made into jelly it will be necessary to clarify it, but for a broth where milk and eggs are to be used this is superfluous. Beat up one fresh laid egg with two tablespoonfuls of rich milk and add to it a cup- ful of the melted jslly•, which should then be boiling hot, stirring it all the time. Season it with salt and pepper, and serve it with thin slices of crisp toast. A broth of oatmeal and beef-tea, or of oatmeal with mutton broth which has been kept free from vegetables, is a suitable beverage for certain invalids. Mix a tablespoonful of oatmeal with a tablespoonful of cold water and add to it a cup of the boiling broth. Let the mixture boil for five minutes, strain it, and serve. It is the greatest folly to give food of any kind to an invalid without some con- sultation csith the attending physician as to the require- ments of the case. Physicians usually give orders as to the diet, if there is not a trained nurse employed, and the rules which he lays down should always be carefully regarded ; the simple beverage Which may bs the most valuable for one may be dangerous to another,

Amongst the new fashions in materials a kind of matalasse cloth in black strikes me as being par- ticularly beautiful as it makes a charming dress. The full bodice and upper sleeves of the dress should be of a dull green silk, the plain skirt and lower sleeves of the matalasse, with shaped bands below the bust of shot green and black velvet, and narrow bands of the same round the sleeves and skirt is very pretty. Among the host of pretty bits of furniture with which modern drawing-rooms are adorned, a screen plays one of the most useful parts. There are many charming designs with which a plain piece of wood may be converted into a choice and elegant ornament. A pretty fashion is to drape coloured silk scarfs over the screen, fastening them here and there with long strings of ribbon of a deeper shade; or, if one has a taste for painting, some perfectly delightful screens of small dimensions may be arrest/Ad from sheets of cathedral glass, coloured in oils with bold sprays of roses or golden-brown chrysanthemums. As a protection from draughts, which penetrate so frequently oven from fast-closed doors and windows, nothing can equal our dearly-. loved screen, and few pieces of furniture add more to the beauty of a room than a well made and neat looking articles of this description.

The old fashioned custom of decorating eggs for presents at Eas,er time, anent the origin of which there is considerable dispute. seems to be gradually dying out. In these days when many of the old and time-honoured customs are ignored this is to be regretted. An artist friend of mine tells me that eggs can be decorated and coloured in a most Interesting and beautiful way by a very simple process. She says that to dye an egg in variegated colours, take five or six odd bits cf bright coloured milk, which should be shreded out. Bind these ravellings round the eggs with a piece of flannel, tack a pin fast and boil in the ordinary fashion. The effect will be that the eggs will look like pieces of marble. To decorate with ornament, monogram or other devices, whatever pattern is drawn upon the eggs with a pen or small brush with oil and the oil allowed to dry before boiling, will appear in the original white of the shell upon the dyed eggs. The effect produced is very pretty. Yours very sincerely, JENNIE.

How To Catch Rheumatism. The plan is simple, and is invariably successful when diligently pursued. Clothe as warmly as possible, with flannels next the skin, and sealskins outermost from November to the beginning of March. Then on the first clear sunny day in March, when the wind is in the north or north-east take off all outer wraps, mantles, capes, sealskins, and the like wear gowns of a light and thin material, and go for a walk in the pa rk, or other open and unprotected place. HOT CROSS-BUNS.

The popular cry of" Hot Cross-Buns" on Good Fri- day, is so familiar to all who have lived in a town, whether large or small, that the reader might incline to wonder. upon the first view of the case, why sO appar- ently trifling a subject should be brought before his notice ; but two considerations may be suggested on this head; first, that ages upon ages have rolled away, and cross-buns have been regularly made at the Paschal season of the year ; and that which is ancient in its origin, will naturally, if only on account of its antiquity attract our curiosity as it passes down the long extended river of time. In the second place, though cross buns will be found to be heathenish in their origin, popish in their progress, and common in their continuance, they nevertheless bear reference to the Cross of Christ, _

Ceorops, one of the kings of Greece, about 16 cen- turies before the Christian era, is said to have first offered up to the Divinity the sacred cross-bread, called a bun, which was made of fine flour and honey. The Prophet Jeremiah, who flourished about 600 years "Lc., notices this kind of offering, when he speaks of the Jewish women at Pathros in Egypt, and of their base idolatry,—the cakes, which they offered up to the moon, the queen of heaven. This cake qr bun is therefore a species of bread,which originally used to be offered to the gods, and it was usually purchased by the worshippers at the entrance of the temple, and taken in by them, and eaten at the feast at the remaining parts of the sacri- fice; to which St. Paul alludes in 1 Con x. 28. It is a remarkable fact, that at Herculaneum were found two small loaves of about five inches in diameter, marked with a cross, within which were four other lines and so, we are told, the bread of the Greeks was marked from the earliest periods. Sometimes it had only four lines altogether, and then it was called " quadra." This bread had rarely any other mark than a cross, which was on purpose to divide and break it more easily. Similar loaves were discovered in a bakehouse at Pompeii. These towns were overwhelmed and destroyed by the voleanic eruption of Mount Vesuvius, A.D. 79. In the course of time, we find the Christian Church using cakes or buns, such as we have already referred to, and consecrating them ; these were bestowed in the church as alms, and likewise to those who, from any impediment, could not receive the host, or consecrated wafer, at the usual time of the celebration of the Lord's supper. These buns were made from the dough, from which the host itself was taken, and they were given by the priest to the people after mass, just before the congregation were dismissed ; and they were kissed before they were eaten. They were also marked with the cross, just as our present Good Friday buns are. This bun is the most popular symbol of the Roman Catholic religion in England that the Reformation has left to us.

Hot cross-buns have the usual form of buns ; but they are inwardly distinguished from other buns by having a sweeter taste ; and outwardly they are known by the mark of the cross,which has been greatly insisted on in Papal worship and devotion from the days of Con- stantine the Great, in the early part of the fourth cen- tury, to the present hour. We see, therefore, that the bun of the ancient Greeks was adopted by the Christians, and used as the only food on the day of the Crucifixion, because it possessed, ready at hand, a symbol of that solemn event. Thus then, the cakes or buns of Good Friday are marked with the sign of the cross, in remembrance of the Crucifixion of our Saviour. The aupellation of" Good," which has been conferred upon this day, is peculiar to the Church of England, and is accounted for by referring to the blessed effects purchased for us by our Lord's sufferings. The ancient title of the day was" Holy Friday" ; and the week in which it happens is still denominated "Holy " or " Passion week."

DR. BARNARDO AND THE STREET

ARABS.

In the "Strand Magazine " Dr. Barnardo tells an interviewer how he came to take up his present occupation. It was the winter of 1886. Together with one or two fellow-students he conducted a ragged school in an old stable. The young student told the oh ildren stories—simple and understandable —and read to them such works as the " Progress." The nights were cold, and the young stu- dents subscribed together—in a practical move—for a huge fire. One night young Barnardo was just about to go when,approaching the warming embers to brace him- self up for the snow outside, he saw a boy lying there. He was in rags, his face pinched with hunger and suffer- ing. " Now then, my boy—it's time to go," said the young medico. " Please, sir, do let me stop." "I can't my lad, it's time to go home. Where do you live ?" " Don't live nowhere, sir." " Nowhere. Where's your father and mother ?" "Ain't got none, sir." , " For the first time in my life," said Dr.Barnardo,as he was telling this incident, "I was brought face to face with the misery of outcast childhood. I questioned the lad. He had been sleeping in the streets for two or three years, he knew every corner of refuge in London. Well, I took him to my lodgings. I had a bit of a struggle with the landlady to allow him to come in, but at last I suc- ceeded, and we had some coffee together. His reply to one question I asked him impressed me more than any- thing else. "Are there many more like you ?" I asked. "Reaps, sir." 'He spoke the truth. He took me to one spot near Houndsditch. There I obtained my first view of real Arab life. Eleven lads—some only nine and ten years of age—lay on the roof of a building. It was a strange sight—the moon seemingly singling out every sleeper for me. Another night we wont together over to the Queen's Shades, near Billingsgate. On the top of a number of barrels; covered with tarpaulin, 73 fellows were sleeping. ,I had the whole lot out for a halfpenny apiece. "By God's help," I cried Inwardly, '"nr help these fellows." Owing to a meeting at Islington my experience got into the daily Press. The late Lord Shaftesbury sent for me, and one • Met at his house at dinner I was chaffed for romancing,', When Lord Shaftesbury went with me to Billingsgate that same night and found 37 boys there he knew the terrible truth. Su we started with 15 or 20 boys, in lodgings, friends paying for them. Then I opened a dilapidated house, once occupied by a stock dealer, but with the help of brother medicos it was cleaned, scrubbed, and white- washed. We begged, borrowed, and very nearly stole . the needful bedsteads. The place was ready, and it I was soon filled with 25 boys. And the work grew—and grew—and grew—you know what it is to-day.

EMINENT DUNCES. It frequently happens that the youth of high scholas- tic attainments do)es not meet with the success in life which his friends predicted for him, while the man dubbed" dunce," or "slow," makes surprising progress. Newton, the greatest of English philosophers, was a lag- gard in his classes when at school. Swift frequently wore the fool's cap. At the Dublin University, "he was by scholars esteemed a blockhead." Goldsmith's fame was grafted upon a boyhood of wholly unrecognised capabilities. At every school we hear of the" inspired idiot "of the future as a "shy, thick, awkward boy," the constant butt of his companions by whom he was thought to be "little better than a fool." Sheridan gave almost no promise in childhood of his future brilliancy. His mother pronounced "Richard Brinsley " to be the "dullest and most hopeless of her sons," and he was sent home from Dr. Whyte's academy with the reputation of being an impenetrable dunce, who wrote "think "for " thing" But he must have been able to think a thing or two oven in those days, for at twenty-six he had written " The School for Scandal." Douglas Jerrold, another and scarcely less keen wit of a more modern school, wrote "Black-eyed Susan" when h 6 was twenty- one, and contributed to " Punch" the immensely popu- lar " Candle Lectures" not long afterwards. But at nine years of age young Jerrold had been scarcely able to read. Gibbon was pronounced" dreadfully dull," and the utmost that was predicted of Hume in his youth was that he " might possibly become a steady merchant." Adana Clarke, afterwards so deeply skilled in Oriental languages and antiquities, was pronounced by his father to he" a grievous dunce," and of Boileau, who became a model for Pope, it was said he was a youth of little un- derstanding. Dryden was a great " numskull." As a boy Walter Scott gave few indications of his coming greatness, and was described by one of his early precep- tors as" the boy that has the thickest skull in the school." Doctor Chalmers was solemnly expelled from the parish school of Saint Andrew's, as an " incorrigible dunce." Ludwig, the famous geometrician, was also sent away from school after four years' ineffectual struggle to learn the common rules of arithmetic.

A TALE OF LONEMOOR.

CHAPTER XXL A GLIMPSE.

Gwen had gone to London. Expelled from Lonemoor, the only home she had ever known, her enemies cruel and relentless, her friends cold and unsympathising, Gwen's thoughts had turned to her old governess, the best and truest friend she had ever possessed. Miss Granger, it will be remembered, had married a poor curate, and now resided in one of the suburbs of the . metropolis. Gwen decided to go to her. "She will find me something to do," the girl thought. "I must earn my own support hereafter. I would never have allowed the Quillets to support me as they have done if I had known." She had unfolded her plan to the old housekeeper when the two had retired to her chamber after the stormy interview with the bailiff. And Mrs. Quillet had approved her regolve. "I suppose it will be best," the old woman said, with a sigh. "I am tired of this continual fighting with the Orkneys. You have no society here, Miss Gwen, no one to talk to, nothing to occupy you. You must stay with Mrs. Myner until we can hear from the sciuire. Then, if he will do nothing for you, you can see about becoming a governess." Gwen made no audible objection, but she had not the slightest intention of waiting to "hear from the squire." Nothing could have induced her to accept charity from a person she had never seen, and whom she believed to be no relation to her. And any aid from Squire Markham she would have termed charity. "I don't think you will hear from the squire, Mrs. Quillet," she said. "There is one thing I must say 'to you before I go. lam very grateful for all you have done for me—for the money you have spent upon my education especially. I shall never cease to be grateful, although I regard those sums spent for rue as simply a loan, which I shall repay one day with interest." "As you like about that, Miss Gwen," replied Mrs. Quillet. "You may feel as independent as you please, and pay the money back if you ever have it to spare; but at the same time you are quite wel- come to it, and John and I don't look for repay- ment. What we did was done from a sense of duty. Your poor mother was a lady, and we couldn't allow her child to be brought up as a servant." 'Gwen stooped and kissed the old woman impul- sively, and then turned away in silence. Mrs. Quillet packed the girl's box while the latter put on her travelling costume. "Miss Gwen," said the housekeeper, when her task had been completed, and they were ready to descmd, "you can't be too particular about your conduct in the great world into which you are going. I don't like young Orkney's looks, nor his unreason- ing pursuit of you. They say he is going up to Lon- don. He will seek you out unless we conceal your address. I shall caution John to tell no one. Be on your guard also." Gwen promised, and the two went below. Gwen departed in the old pony-chaise with the old butler, as we have mentioned. Young Orkney made an attempt to follow her, to urge anew his suit; but his father detained him, imploring him not to dis- grace his family, and threatening, if he went after the girl, to disown him as his son, and to oast him off with a shilling. Believing that he could readily discover Gwen's retreat, Claxton made a virtue of necessity, and sub- mitted to his father's commands.

The butler drove briskly to Penistone. As they passed over the moor, he gave Gwen counsel similar to that his wife had given her, urging her to keep her refuge a secret, and asked her how much money she possessed.

"1 have three pounds left from my school allow- ance," answered Gwen. "That will take me to Lon- don."

"But you must not arrive there penniless. Miss Gwen, here are fifty pounds I got out of my wife's tea-caddy. She meant it for your winter outfit and the like. You can repay it some day if you wish, but I insist upon you taking it now l" He drew from his pocket a small canvas bag filled with gold coins. Gwen hesitated. Her pride tempted her to refuse it, but she reflected that she might need it, and the butler's offer of the sum as a loan saved her pride. "I will take it, and thank you, Mr. Quillet," she said. "I shall repay it, and all the money you have spent upon me. I shall deny myself every luxury until you are repaid. But money can never pay you for your kindness, nor can I cease to be grateful for it l" There were tears in her purple-dusk eyes that attested her sincerity. She put the little canvas bag in her pocket, and Mr. Quillet occupied the remainder of the drive in giving her such respectful and kindly counsels as a trustworthy old family servant might well have given his inexperienced young mistress under the lace circumstances.

They arrived at Penistone in time to catch the London express. Mr. Quillet placed his charge in a ladies' compartment, first-class, put her ticket in her hand, and bade her good bye. .

He was sorely tempted th go up to town with her. I She was too young, too beautiful, and too innocent to travel alone. But his enemies—he counted the Ork- neys and the lawyer as such in these days—were at Lonemoor in his absence. He was anxious to return to them and his wife. Gwen's destiny seemed to have been taken out of his hands. It might be best to let her battle with life at once. She must fight for herself hereafter. And so he let her go away alone. He waited until the train had steamed out of the station and was fairly on its way southward, and then he re-entered his chaise and drove homewards. Gwen settled herself in a seat by one of the win- dows and gave herself up to her reflections. She had three fellow passengers who had entered the compartment before herself—an elderly lady, a little girl, and a maid servant. The child slept, the elderly lady read a novel, and the maid looked out upon the landscape. None of them paid any attention to Gwen after the first scrutiny at her apparel. Our young heroine had been very miserable at Lone-moor. Her life had seemed for ever darkened. But the excitement and novelty of her journey, the complete breaking off of the old life, the uncer- tainty of the new, were especially healthful for her. She felt her interest in life returning. True, her lover had proved false—so she thought—true, her origin was disgraceful—true, that she was friend-

less—y—et, after all, Providence had placed her on this earth—had sent her all these trials, and she ought to bear them bravely and cheerfully. The Cross here, the Crown hereafter!

Not all this did she say to herself at once, how- ever. She had an heroic soul, but the lesson of resignation is not easily learnt. She was one to feel deeply, and deep feelings are not soonest dissipated. Her fellow travellers, with that reserve and exclu- s;yeness which finds its perfect growth in England, dIPI not address one word to her throughout the day. She might have been a stock or stone for all the interest they displayed towards her, or the notice they took of her. She was equally silent. The train steamed into the London terminus just at nightfall. A slight mist was falling. The guard assisted Gwen out last of all, and motioned to a cabman, to whose tare Gwen committed herself. She took her travelling bag inside the vehicle, and the driver put her trunk upon the box beside him.

"What E-tddress ?" asked-the cabman. Gwen had kept up an active correspondence with her former governess for years. She replied promptly— "Number eighty, Northumberland-terrace, Queen- street, Notting-hill." The cab started. As it moved out of the station another cab entered hastily to catch the night express about to start northward.

Gwen, looking from the window, half hidden by the little flapping curtain, beheld the occupant of that other cab, and her heart gave a bound, and she sank back white and faint. For the occupant of that cab was Ronald Chilton the lover who had wooed her on the Yorkshire moor, and who, hearing her story, had abandoned her. He looked years older than when she had last seen him. His face was graver than she had known it. A new nobility seemed throned upon it. Fair and handsome, he looked to her yearning eyes like a demigod I He did not see her. His cab passed on in haste. Gwen, finding strength, sprang to the window and looked after him for one last glimse. She saw him leap from his cab upon the platform; then her own vehicle rolled out of the station, and he was lost to her view. CRAFT= XXIL

(Applause.) However, they had I,7o'ich to thank Professor Marshall for, and therefore he ssiraeT take that opportunity on behalf of the Viaduct In- fer tise, to thank Profeseor Marshall and his colleagues, "e monetary assistance which they had been instru- oeetttal in gaining for them. k Applause.) He would now latulate the successful students upon the achieve- to rihey had made. But whilst doing so he wished thet,,V-und those students who had not gained prizes that 10;Y. Ought not to suffer disappointment to affect them, seer was a fact that often the best scholars in a school ree: through some slip or small mietake debarred from aps'iving a prize And then the successful students he theti'ld warn not .to become too lax or in any way relax the r,efferts and energies, because of the distinctions IcZoad received. The present was a time when it was tilili;Y necessary for the youth of Enaland to follow up less.,„ studies and become possessed of extensive know- vile" se that they may better meet the competition titnteh abounded on all hands. (Applause.) It was a to „e„new when the youth of England were called upon assV,tane knowledge and obtain scholastic achievements eossess themselves of all the advantages which ineee:l2li cal Institutes offered.so that in the face of the ever tiats'asine and ever extending competition of continen- ts, s-atione,they might by their superior wisdom maintain the commercial altitude of England amongst re,„'S'ations of the world. (Loud applause.) It would be En-,7 be re (I by some in that room that 40 or 50 years ago. the "d held the monopoly of many of the markets of of World. (Hear, hear.) That day had goneahe times arZr monopoly amid unhampered trade had vanished, its place had been set up a system of keen and imsurmountable competition competition Would require to be met and fought with bythose

possessing advantages and qualities even greater than tne qualifications which many of their continental com- petitors possessed. At one time they used to supply the Americans with a variety of machines and mechanical fittings, but America not only supplied herself now, bet had become a formidable competitor of England's. In 1830 they projected their first railway and they had had the honour of manufacturing and despatching to America the rails. And so they could fairly claim to the distinclion of having been immensely instrumental in the opening of the first American railway. Since 1830, however, the Americans had progressed considerably in the manufacture of iron. He was told that they pro- duced nine and a half millions of tons in 1890, against seven and three quarter millions produced by the foundries of this country. Then there were the Ger- man competitors to be taken into account. England used to supply them with axles and other parts of the mechanicism of engines, but now they were in a position to supply themselves and also compete with England in all the markets of the world. And they were succeeding in their competition chiefly by the aid of underpaying, or paying less than mechanics were paid in England. (Hear, hear.) Then there was Belgium to be accounted for, too. Be was sorry to see that the Belgians were sending iron and steel at a much cheaper rate than they could manufacture it in England ; they were sending it to the English markets and under selling the English merchants. He had been asked how was it that in, Sheffield cheaper Belgium steel railway axles could be obtained than English ones, and the only conclusion to arrive at again was that Belgian workmen were paid at a much lower rate thanthe English artizans. (Applause.) Therefore it seemed to him hardly the time for the work- men of England to agitate for the eight hours working day. (Hear, hear.) They were being borne down upon by a strength of opposition and competition which they could no longer ignore it was a competition which in- volved the welfare of the British nation, and it was a competition which they could only hope to meet by fighting their foreign competitors with the weapons they used. (Loud applause.) Professor MILNES MARSUALL, who was then called upon to distribute the prizes,expressed the great pleasure which he felt in being brought into contact with the large number of successful scholars who were there that evening to receive their well-earned prizes. (Applause.) At the outset he wished to heartily congratulate the suc- cessful students; but he would impress upon them tne great necessity of not allowing their recently-earned distinctions to lessen their enthusiasm or in any ,way interfere with the zest which they had infused into their studies hitherto. They must not be contented with the laurels they had won; they must only regard them as a result which should stir them on to better and higher attempts—(applause)—attempts at the attain- ment of better things in the future. Upon the successful student lay now a more responsible task than hitherto, for they would be looked to as students of higher courses than they had now gone through with success. (Ap- plause.) Alluding to students who had not taken prizes he advised them not to feel liscouraged, for although they had not been awarded marks of distinction, there was no doubt that many of them had only been debarred from receiving prize.; through a slip or error in a minor detail. (Applause.) It was impossible that everyone should receive a prize. Professor Huxley he thought it was, had said that it did not matter how often one tumbled in this life provided you were not dirtied by the tumble. It certainly did not matter how often one tumbled so long as it was not ignominiously. It was an admitted fact that most great men had only attained their celebrity by taking advantage of the lessons which they learned by their frequent tumbles. (Applause.) Referring to University Extension Lectures the Professor said he did not think the original motive of those lectures was understood and appreciated by the people of Lancashire as it deserved to be. The object of the movement was a two-fold one. First it aimed at assisting those who found it impossible to take up a residence of two and three years at the University to obtain the chance of securing education upon subjects which would be beneficial to them, and secondly they were lectures which were given in accordance with a demand made by the public for a more equal distribu- tion of the pivilege of education, rendered somewhat exclusive by reason of social standards. They were first started by the Cambridge University, and then by the Oxford University, and then ultimately by their own University of Lancashire—the Victoria University. (Applause.) They began seven years ago with one single course ; now at the end of the seventh year they found that there were no less than 99 courses. Year by year those courses were in- creasing. They found however, that the allocation of the grant was certainly not altogether a matter of satis- faction, for out of the 99 courses he had mentioned 66 were held in Yorkshire. He did not deny that Yorkshire deserved all the good things she could secure, but it struck him that Lancashire was a little remiss if she was going to allow Yorkshire to enjoy her share of good things. (Applause.) However, he thought the matter greatly rested in their own hands, as to whether they should have another course or not. If there was an audience to he obtained, and the people of Earlestown were really desirous that the course should be held there, it merely rested with them to so explain the matter to those who held the sinews of war—the money—and he believed they would secure what they desired. (Ap- plause.) The Professor then alluded to the choice of subjects at the Institute. He advised them to go in for as great a variety as possible, and he advised individuals not to merely select a subject because it would be useful to him in his particular occupation or profes- sion. The best thing to do was to select a subject as far away from their everyday occupation as possible—some- thing new to them and something which they could make:a " hobby" of. He believed hobbies were the most healthful and useful thtngs a man could have. They took his mind from the groove of his own every- day occupation and opened his interest to other things which were the fields of the occupation of others and which he knew nothing of. A hobby broadened the understanding and sympathies, and interests, and often leads a man's mind to a science or an art which he was particularly fitted for. (Applause.) The professor then proceeded to distribute the prizes.

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

MONDAY.

In the House of Commons on Monday evening Mr. GrAnsToNn said the Government deemed it necessary that the second reading of the Home Rule Bill should be moved on Thursday. April 6, when the House would re- assemble after the Easter recess. Mr. A. J. BALFOUR moved the resolution of which he had given notice, de- claring—" That the action of the Executive in condoning 'serious offences and their failure to support and enforce the law are calculated to resuscitate the system of terror- ism and intimidation which formerly prevailed in Ire- land, and to bring the administration of the law into contempt." Mr. JOHN MORLEY replied in detail to the charges made by Mr. Balfour, and Mr. GLADSTONE spoke at some length in vindicatioq of the Irish administration of the Government. After a speech from Lord R. CHURCHILL the House divided, with the result that the vote of censure was defeated by 319 to 272—majority for the Government, 47._.___

TUESDAY.

In the House of Com-M-o-n-s—o-n-rPuesday afternoon, Mr. BENN said he had found that a number of the signatures to three petitions which he had been asked to present against the Local Veto Bill were forgeries. The Speaker suggested that the matter should be brought under the notice of the Committee on Petitions. Mr. MLNDELLA, replying to a question put by Colonel Vincent as to the great decrease in our foreign trade in the six months ending with February as compared with the correspond lug period in 1889.90, said the comparison was mislead- ing, as the six months three years ago was a period of great inflation, whilst the other period was one of de- pressed and low prices. As to an alleged simultaneous increase of 115,000 trade unionists without employment, no such report had been made to the Labour Department , of the Board of Trade. The HOUR, went into Committee of Supply, and amongst other things dealt with esti- mates relating to the Royal palaces and the Houses of Parliament. In regard to. the latter, demands were made by hon. members for an inquiry as to the necessity of increased accommodation, but Mr. SHAW-LEPKVItH said it would be well to wait and see if under the flame Rule Bill there were to be fewer Irish members in the House. At the evening sitting Mr. E. H. Bayley moved s resolution declaring that it was incumbent on the Gov- ernment to provide at the public expense an effective lifeboat service on the coasts of the United Kingdom. Mr. Mundella deprecated the discouraging of the benev- olent action of the Royal National Lifeboat Institution, to whose usefulness he paid a high compliment, and the motion was defeated by 108 to 67. WEDNESDAY. In the House of Commons on Wednesday Mr. T. W. RUSSELL complained that a London newspaper In its Parliamentary sketch had spoken of him as a " tireless naercenry of Unionism." Mr. GLADSTONE said such criticism deserved the most vehement condemna- tion. He left it to the discussion of the hon. member to move whether the article constituted a breach of privi- lege. After discussion Mr. Russell thanked the Prime Minister for the generous way in which be had met the question and the incident closed. On the motion of Mr. Gladstone that the House should meet at noon to- day, Mr. GOSEHEN charged the Government with mono- polising the time. Mr. CHAPLIN, accusing Ministers of • unconstitutonal action, was called to order by the I SPEAKER. Mr. GLADSTONE, after a reference to the shilly-shally speech of Mr. Goschen, withdrew his • motion. Mr. P. A. MUITIGH moved the second reading of the Evicted Tenants (Ireland) Bill. Sir T. LEA moved its rejection. A long debate fdlowed. In the course of it Mr. JOHN MORLEY supported the second reading. At half-past five o'clock the debate was adjourned.

They were very glad in being able to find, and to induce a gentleman to come among them, whose very name would be an intro- duction to any Free Church or Unitarian congregation throughout the country. (Applause.) He was a des- cendant of a stock well known to Nonconformity, but his own qualities were quite suffcient to ensure for him their regard. (Applause.) He had, previous to coming to Warrington, occupied positions both in Liverpool and Manchester with credit to himself and with very great good to the people amongst whom he had laboured. In Warrington, just now, they were being criticised and , called to order by a very estimable man, the presentj Rector of Warrington, a man of whom he would speakl in terms of the highest esteem and regard, a man who knew his duty as a clergyman amongst his people. But he (the speaker) must form his own opinion as to the advisability of the course he has taken in his Lenten services. In his instructions to the people he has been "holding forth" on the different forms of Nonconform- ity in the town and had taken them one by one. They ought to thank 'Mr. Monks for the very quiet, and he thought, sufficient manner of noticing the remarks upon the Unitarian position, in last week's public press. (Ap- plause.) He did not think that the Unitarians would wish to take up any theological disputes. The Rector of Warrington must know that throughout the world there were something over 300 different forms of religious be- liefs, and when any man could assume to himself to say which body is to have the right to choose which is right and which is wrong, he did not envy his spirit. The Rector might want a little information about the year 1662, when the noble band of men, clergymen of the Church, seceded from that Church rather than sub- scribe to certain articles. The then Rector of Warrington found his religious home on the site of the present chapel in Cairo-st—(applause) —where he ministered a great number of years. He passed on to say that they, as Unitarians—and he dared say the Rector would be astounded to hear it— cared very little for doctrine. They looked more to the pore and simple faith which was given to them by their great exemplar Jesus Christ. (Hear, hear.) An article of their faith was that they should chow forth their faith by their works, rather than by correct doctrine. The rector paid them the compliment of saying that they did some good philantropic work in the town. It was pleasant to hear even that although they suffered under such " awful errors." (Laughter.) He (the speaker) appealed to them to try earnestly to show their inheri- tance to those great and good men who had gone before them, and in the words of Paul, try to show that" in the way that some called heresy so iball they worship the God of their fathers." Before he sat down he would in the name of the congregation offer Mr. Drummond as hearty a welcome as Lancashire people could give. (Applause.) A short time ago they were called together to speed the parting guest now they were welcoming the new one, who he hoped would be among them a long time, and they would also join in the welcome, Mrs. Drummond. He hoped their connection with them would be pleasant on both sides. (Applause.) Letters of regret for inability to be present were then read, and amongst them were letters from the Revs. F. K. Freeston and Steinthal. The Rev. J. E. ODGERS said that he endorsed all that had been said of Mr. Drummond in the letters just read from so many of his brother ministers. He also knew the Warrington congregation, that they had endeared themselves personally and collectively to ministers whose ministry they had been privileged to enjoy. Be felt is though he knew them, as he knew so intimately Richard Pilchard, and Frank K. Freeston, and he could promise Mr. and Mrs. Drummond they they would do the same to them as they had done to the former minis- ters. In getting Mr. Drummond, the congregation got a man who had won his spurs in a very arduous field of labour in Liverpool, and also in Cross-st., Manchester, where he had developed strong powers of organisation and leadership. He looked forward with the utmost confi- dence to the connection which was that evening inaugu- rated. Mr. J, R. BEARD congratulated the congregation of Cairo-st., on the minister they had obtained, and he wished the blessing of God upon his workin Warrington. Mr. Drummond was as full of energy and devotion to his work as a man could possibly be, and he (the speaker) hoped he would find a co-worker in every one of the congregation. He sometimes thought that a minister was most useful, who did least himself and got other people to work for him. The Rev. L. Jams, a fellow student of Mr. Drummond spoke in high praise of him in his younger days. Mr. W. H. Wooncocx, on behalf of the Sunday school, welcomed Mr. Drummond to Warrington, and hoped he would give his attention to that institution. The Rev. W. 11. Deummorno, who was warmly re- ceived, thanked the members of the congregation for the cordial reception they had given him, and also for the kind way in which they had invited him to become their minister. When they invited him he could not say no. He would like to say that when they had such confidence in a man it seemed to enhance the sense of responsibility with which he undertook the work, and also gave him greater faith and confidence in his own power to work,if he only did it in the rigjit spirit. This was the third welcome of ministers he had attended in that room. He had attended the welcomes of his fellow students the Revs. Priestley Prime and F.K. Freeston and now it was Isis own. It was his earnest desire to become the friend of everyone both in the chapel and in the school. He believed very strongly in pastoral work. This work would take a considerable amount of time, but it was time extremely well spent. He hoped they would let him into their homes not as a stranger or a distinguished guest, hut on the platform of common friendship. He did not like being taken into the parlour when they lived in the kitehen. He thought that a congregation with a ihni sgt ory se positionc o ni clo tool l ngoonned in the works.tc wfin houghtope hoped o tthaek a ye wloeua Idd- rise to their responsibilities in this matter, and try to make the town a little brighter and better for men and women to live in. There were many ways in which they might develop into ways pf usefulness, and it would be his earnest desire to do something in this way. He hoped for the sympathy and prayers of the congregation. He thought nobody could worship in that historic chapel with its grand traditions still living in it,without feeling uplifted by it. He was also gratified by the musical ser- vice in the chapel. He hoped that his connection with the place begun this week would last a long time. (Ap- plause.) He did not wish to go through the experience of parting with his congregation again, as he had done in Manchester. He concluded by hoping that they would work well together,that the school and chapel would soon be too small for the good work. " BOWIN' TIME!"

Wot is it poo's my sperrits up, An' makes me lung to hey a sup 0' eaur Owd Cheermon's "loving cup ?" • Its Bowin' Time !" (Eh, lads! he duz keep gradely stuff! An' beaut a shadow of a puff, Yo caunt tell wen yo'n had enuff I I'th' Bowin Time !) Like growin' lads, aw'm gettin' on; My Winter mopin's welly gone, An'—fact—aw'm quite another mon Its Bowin' Time ! Aw'm lungin"t see Owd Bashaw's skill; An'" Yethart " workin' wi' a will, To gie th' Owd Skoller's luck a pill— I'th' Bowin' Time! Aw'm switherin"t watch "Uncle James" Play one o' his owd-fashiened games— Short marks, an' sundry other skames ! l'th' Bowin' Time! An' truth to tell yo, aw shud like To tak' a lesson, heaw to strike. Fro Bontinental Jack or Ike! I'th' Bowin' Time! 'Twud do me good to see "Owd Bob," Weu gettin' warm, to scratch his nob. An' bias (or woss !) a stiffish job! Pth' Bowin' Time!

HILL CLIFF BAPTIST CIIIMICH could be traced distinctly to soon after that time, and he believed it was beyond doubt that there were large numbers of Baptists in this country who might be re- garded as their ancestors. Indeed, the Rector admitted that under different names in the second, third, and fourth centuries, and even down to the 16th century, Baptists existed. Was it true to say that these people did not belong to the Church? He believed so. Let him take a few instances of that interesting fact. About the year 600 A.D., Austin, who was sent to England by the Pope, found a society of Christians at Bangor, con- sisting of 2,100 persons. They were destroyed because they refused to baptise infants at the Pope's command. In the time of William the Conqueror they found that the Baptists had spread so rapidly that the Archbishop of Canterbury, to quote the words of Lanfranc, "see- jag that many of the nobles as well as of the poor had adopted their sentiments, wrote a book against them in which he complained as Archbishop Egbert did of the Cathari (Puritans), that they were very pernicious to the Catholic faith, for they maintained their opinions by authority of Scripture '—a great crime in those days, and still a great inconvenience to those who preferred the traditions and customs of men to the command of God. But the Baptists flourished in spite of the Arch- bishop's book, and therefore the King was induced to issue an edict that "those who denied the Pope should not trade with his subjects." When at the time of the Establishment of the Church of England by Henry VIII., and he himself undertook to settle what the people should believe, he drew up a set of articles of religion. One of those articles stated that the opinions of the Anabaptists were detestable heresies. They must remember those people immersed only those who had believed. They ignored the fact of infant baptism, and demanded believers' baptism. They claimed precisely what they claimed to-day; that persons should be im- mersed on a profession of faith, although those might have been sprinkled when infants. Those people ex- isted in the 16th century in large numbers, and no one, so far as they knew, ever said they belonged to the Church of that time. The Rector had also told them that they held "That Christ only died for a few elect of God, elected to everlasting life, and that the rest were formed by God for everlasting damnation." He called it "a grievous error or mistake." So they did. He said "it was an awful idea to have." So it was. THEY DID NOT CROSS SWORDS with him on that question. There might have been,and there might still be a few who held that doctrine, and who imagined, therefore, that they and they alone were the chosen people. It was possible for men even in the 19th century to hold error ; but the Baptists as a body would repudiate with horror such a view of the scrip- tures. If he were asked for the Baptist view, he should point to and quote the 17th of the 39 Articles in the book of Common Prayer. The Church of England and the Baptists were therefore at one in their interpretation of election as taught in the scriptures. Now let them take the Rector's view of their interpretation of baptism, and their position with regard to the constitution of the church. Very naturally his interpretation of the Church determined his view of Baptism. He believed in a State Church. He believed in a church that should include wheat and tares ; a church that should have within its meshes good and bad fish. Aiayone who ac- cepted the idea of a National Church must of course seek to have each individual In the community within its pale, for logically carried out, this idea of a National Church must include all, good and bad. Sprinkling the Infant, therefore, became the medium of entrance into the church. By the sprinkling, every child was made ,an inheritor of the Kingdom of Heaven. In the few drops of water that trickled from the finger tips, there mustof necessity be conveyed the grace of regeneration. A mechanical act was supposed to produce a purely spiritual result. A man was empowered by the Bis- hop's touch to come between the child and the Saviour, and to do for it what no saint, however distinguished for piety and intelligence, without ordination, was compe- tent to do. MEN AND WOMEN CALLED SPONSORS were made to promise for the child what no living man was capable of performing for another. And as if to prevent any mistake, the child was taught to say, in answer to the question, " Who gave you this name ?" , " My godfathers and godmothers in my baptism, where- in I was made a member of Christ, a child of God, and an inheritor of the Kingdom of Heaven." That was the way the National Church was built up; and all that was necessitated by the desire to perpetuate a State-controlled religion. Baptism, therefore, came in fora due measure of praise. They, the Baptists. rightly emphasized bap- tism, the Rector thought. But his views and theirs were not the same. To begin with they did not hold adult baistism. Over and over again had they:to empha- size that distinction. What they did hold, was be- liever's baptism ; which was a very different thing. All thatthey asked was that baptism—and by that they meant immersion—should be put where Christ put it. They did not make it, nor ask for it to be the door into the church, but a personal manifesta- tion of faith in Christ's atonement and forgive- ness of sin. Baptism did not produce that faith. In conclusion he knew of no body of Protestant Christians which had such valid and weighty reasons for maintain- ing its denominational existence as their own. If they were not so successful in the furtherance of those great objects as they desired, if through their remissness, or notwithstanding their vigilance, unbelievers gained admission, so that they could nct boast of the purity of the church, that fact did not derninish, but rather aug- mented the importance of their testimony being main- tained as a barrier to THE INROADS OF GODLESS MEN. Let them listen to the teaching of the English Church Union regarding baptism. The East Budleigh (Devon) peasantry were thus instructed in the sheet almanac : —" Rejoice in your newly-baptised bany. He is more yours than he was when you took him to Church. Before his baptism there was a barrier between you as wide as Christianity itself ; for you were a Christian and he was not. Now you are both Christians." Such teaching also came in with regard to the Lord's Supper avd to burial; for even the bread and wine acquired a sacredness which they did not possess before ; and even when death had severed soul and body this ritualism laid its clutches upon the lifeless remains. If the rite of baptism had been administered by a " priest," whatever the character of a man may be, he was committed to the dust as" a dear brother in hope of a glorious resur- rection." But if not, all the virtues a man might possess would not secure for him the right and privilege of Christian burial, so far as the services of the Church of England were concerned. Did not this spirit of ritual- ism be the authority of the Prayer-book, dog a man's steps all through his life? Did it not bend over his cradle, and mutter its falsehood in the ear of the infant? As the child stood at its mother's knee, did it not through the catechism instil its poison into his opening mind? Did it not meet him when he approached the communion table, and change a simple evening meal of bread and wine into a superstitious rite? In this pro- fessed Protestant Church they would find clergy who would give them minute directions for the use of the tcioonnfsestsoiotnhaelv. irTgliineyanwdoutoldtfihendsatinhetsm. uTthteeyrinwg ioulndvoficnad- the " priest" put into the place of God. The whole system, mitINSVIsaTS to bPCOIDEtsrIi d as to ER O THE feared, O it as o PRIEST, and and to make him and through him " the Church" the only medium through which God could he approached. P In the face of these thine s the Baptists had but one testimony to give. A distinguished historian had said " that it is the singular and honoured privilerse of the Baptists to have repudiated from their earliest history all coercive power over the conscience and actions of men with reference to religion." The Baptists of to-day could therefore but take up the same position. They did not believe, nor have they believed in the right of any Church t. call itself " the" authority in questions of faith and practice. At the cost of much suffering and blood their forefathers struggled for the doctrine of the headship of Christ. They followed in their footsteps so far as their witness was concerned. They wanted no persecution or legal restriction. They claimed to have the right to speak the truth as they conceived it was revealed in the Bible. If no bishop's hands had been put upon their heads, they had the blessing, of the Bishop of all the Churches. From Him they have received the consecrating fire,

GOVERNMENT -INQUIRIES AT

ST. HELENS.

SEWAGE, HOSPITAL, AND LIBRARY SCHEMES. Major-General C. Phipps Carey, ILE., an inspector of the Local Government Board, held inquiries at the St. Helens Town Hall, on Friday, into applica- tions made by the St. Helens Corporation to borrow money to complete the main sewer and construct outfall works at the Double Locks ; to erect addi- tional pavilions at the Infectious Diseases Hospital' for gasworks purposes ; and for promoting a branch library at Farr. There were present the Mayor (Mr. A. Sinclair), Alderman Gamble, C. 8., Alderman J. C. Gamble, Alderman R. Pilkington, Alderman T. Cook, Alderman J. M'Bryde, Councillors J. Forster, H. Martin, A. J. Foote, J. P. Mearns. and J. Burchall, and Messrs. B. A. Dromgoole, G. Welsby, W. J. Jeeves (town-clerk), G. J. C. Broom (borough surveyor), S. Glover (gasworks manger), A. Lmcaster (librarian), J. Brown (borough treasurer), Drs, M'Nicoll and Robertson (medical officers), Mr, Carter Bell, and Mr. G. T. Lee (deputy town- clerk). The Town-clerk explained in detail the features of the sewage scheme, and Mr. Broom, the borough surveyor, described the present Main intercepting sewer from Spray's Bridge to Ashcroft's Bridge. He said it was proposed to continue it from the latter point along the valley, over the Rain- ford Brook, under the Blackbrook branch and Havdock branch of the L. and N.-W. Railway Company, and then on to the land (10 acres) which had been purchased near the Double Locks for the purpose of dealing with the sewage. The new portion would be 5,323 'feet long. The gradient would be 1 in 1,483 for 3,100 feet and 1 in 1,684 for 2,223 feet. At the land, which was the only piece available, it was proposed to erect six tanks, each 153 feet long by 33 feet wide and 8 feet deep, capable of containing 168,210 gallons, making a total of 1,009,000 gallons, which at the present time is nearly as much as the dry weather flow. The sewage would be allowed to flow into the tanks alternately, each one being filled before they commenced on another. He estimated the cost of carrying out the whole of the works at £25,5000, made up as tollows :—Sewer from Ashcroft's Bridge to Boardman's-lane, £11,502 ; from Boardman's-lane to outfall works, a short distance, £3,598, which was rather expensive ; tanks, £9,400 ; and machinery and filtering process, £l,OOO. The Town-clerk (Mr. W. J. Jeoves) said ho had not heard of any opposition to the four schemes. He then stated that the borrowing powers of the Corporation were £320,000. Dr. R. ArNicoll, senior medical officer for the borough, said since the construction of the first portion there had been a general improvement in the health of the borough and a great diminution in the number of cases of typhoid fever. Dr. Robertson, B.Sc. assistant medical officer, said that from the fact that Chemical waste heaps which were forty or fifty years old were still giving off drainage, if all the chemical works of St. Helens were to be shut up at once it was probable that this chemical action would go on for fifty or one hundred years longer. The the etrosw passed under by yte lheeaakpasg, eand o t a t into many points in the sewers and were likely to do so. then chemicals waste a Mr. Carter Bell said he had traced the sewage three miles down the stream and found it unchanged. He had inspected the experimental tanks, and found what he should call putrified sewage. The chemicals in the St. Helens sewage were far in excess of what were re- quired for purification, and he should not tear to turn raw sewage down the, brook. In St. Helens he found the very chemicals which in other places were used specially for precipitation. Evidence was then taken in the matter of the exten- sion of the Infectious Diseases Hospital. It was proposed to erect two blocks so as to accommodate twenty-four beds and purchase an additional acre of land. The cost would be £4,000. The plans had been drawn out and met the wishes of Dr. Thorn, of the Local Government Board. There would be an area of forty feet from the wards to the boundary wall, and as a street came next, it would be seventy feet to the nearest house. The other two inquiries were :—To borrow £B5O for the purchase of land and the erection of a branch pub- lic library at Parr ; and for the erection of tar-distilling works at the gasworks, at a cost of £2,500.

The Rev. W. 11. Drummond commenced his ministry at Cairo-st. on Sunday, when he took for his text, "The spirit of God dwelleth in you," 1. Cur. ill. 16, and "Now ye are the body of Christ and severally members there- of," 1. Cor. xii. 27. He said that religion in recent years had been on a long journey but it had returned to its maly safe resting-place, retiring from its outward defences, that it might control and inspire the inner man of the heart just in proportion as men kept the windows of the soul transparent and left some space for God with- in, free from false ambition or the corrosion of evil thoughts. That was the kernel of religion which could not be destroyed. If religion was to be much to them it must be in that way. And for this reason : faith had long been identified with a certain body of knowledge partly speculative, partly historical and scientific, with- out which it was supposed it could not be. Now, that partnership had been dissolved. One fact after another to which men of a former generation attached religious importance had yielded t) the pressure of investigation. Let them transfer the emphasis of their religion from outward observance or a pious routine of conduct to those recesses of their naturo which could be open to the eye of 'God alone. Some men were not helped at all by being told to look into their own hearts. The divine secret might be hidden there, but they did not possess the key to unlock it. Faith was preserved in strength and purity, nGt by keeping it locked away in their own hearts, for solitary prayer and meditation. Without these it was true they could not live at all, but they ex- posed it to great peril if they withdrew it from the sphere of human sympathy, and the kindling influence of the common realization of the truth of God. In the evening, Mr. Drummond preaching an interest- ing sermon appropriate to the season to a large congre- gation iook for Ids text the account of Christ's entry into Jerusalem, Luke xix. 37-40. Mr. E. Wilmot Gaudy presided at the organ.

AN ENORMOUS PIECE OF COAL

An enormous cob of cannel coal, from the Wigan Junction Colliery, has arrived by train at the Alexandra Dock, Liverpool, for shipment to Chicago. It is said to be the largest block of coal ever dug from the earth, and weight over 12 tons. In getting this cob to the surface many men have been employed, and it took nine months to hew it out of the seam. The cost of obtaining it was £l,ooo—about £B3 per ton. When raised the cob was enclosed in a case of planks, the weight of cob and case being 13 tons 11 cwt. It was placed on board the steamer Philadelphian by means of ropes and chains attached to four pully purchase blocks with a 6in. wire rope span. M a precaution against the masts giving way under the great strain of the lifting gear attached to them, preventive guys were placed fere and aft.

TliE LIQUOR QUESTION IN NEW ZEALAND.:

EXTRAORDINARY INCIDENT. The temperance party in New Zealand lately gained a great triumph at Sydenharn, Christ church, where a teetotal bench refused to renew any of the licences. Their elation has been rather damped by a judgment just delivered by the Supreme Court, to whom the ag- grieved publicans appealed. The court decided that the wholesale closing of the Stklepham hotels, without regard to the rights of the applicants or the requirements of the public, was a vicious miscarriage of justice, and directed that the applications must be dealt with one by one on their merits. In addition to this, three out of the four judges who heard the appeal have "declared that avowed prohibitionists are morally dil,qualified from acting OD licensing committees."
