THE SHOCKING DISCOVERY IN A WIDNES HOTEL.

===== 0002601_18930401_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
OPENING OF THE INQUEST.

On Saturday afternoon, 31r. Brighouse, county coroner, opened an inquest at the Widnes Police Court on the body of a newly-born female child which was found in the Commercial Hotel, Apple- ton, near Widnes, on Thursday evening last. The facts which have up to the present come to the knowledge of the police are as follow :—On Saturday last a woman named Sweeney, who is between forty and fifty years of age, came over to Widnes from the North of Ireland, and went to Mr. Samuel Hurst, landlord of the Commercial Hotel, and applied for a situation as domestic servant. She represented herself as a married woman living apart from her husband, and Mr. Hurst engaged her. On Wednesday morning she was called about six o'clock, but a considerable time elapsed before she came downstairs. She then complained of feeling ill, and lay down on the sofa. Mr. Hurst's suspicions were aroused, and he sent for a midwife, who came and saw the woman. The midwife did not stay, and on Thursday certain information coming to the knowledge of Superintendent Keighley, he had the hotel searched by several constables. The result was that the body of the which is fully developed, was discovered wrapped in an apron in a lumber room, which is close to the bed- room occupied by the prisoner. At Saturday afternoon's inquiry only formal evidence of the finding of the body was adduced, and in order to give the woman time to recover and the completion of the post-mortent, the inquest was then adjourned until April 5.

RUNCORN CRICKET CLUB. ANNUAL DINNER.

!Of)

The annual dinner of the members of Runcorn Cricket Club, took place at Wilsen's Hotel on Friday night last week. The dining room had been taste- fully decorated by Mr. Steele, draper. Mr. C. Dutton, the proprietor, had provided an excellent repast, to which ample justice was done. Mr. S. Taylor, the president of the club took the head of the table, and the Rev. Dr. Gastaldi occupied the vice. chair. Amongst those present were Captsin G. Ashton, Messrs. J. R. Frith, W. B. Frith, A. Nergle, J. Booth, Wynne, W. Carroll, A. Crosby, H. Wright, Cyril Chant- ler, W. Jones. G. S. Frith; James Taylor, Annett, Bate, Galley, and Woods. The President proposed the health of the Queen and Royal Family which was duly honour- ed. The Army, Navy, and Volunteers, was the next toast also given by the President, coupled with the name of Captain Ashton, who responded. Mr. J. R. Frith gave the toast of " The President." He said it was the anxious endeavour of Mr. Taylor to help forward every good work, and whatever he put his hand to he performed thoroughly. In the welfare of the rising generation he especially took a deep and active interest, and to Runcorn Cricket Club he had ever been a true friend, and an ardent supporter. The ' toast was received with musical honours. The President in reply, said it was always a pleasure to hila to further any good cause in Runcorn, whether for young or old. (Hear, hear.) He was confident that Runcorn Cricket Club had a brilliant future before it. He was pleased to know that there were many who gave their attention to cricket and other games, such as foot- ball and lawn tennis, which tended to develop them in a physical as well as a mental sense,. He was pleased to hear that the Runcorn Cricket Club were advancing their position, and that they had fixtures with stronger clubs than they had competed with in previous years. He should like to see more interest taken in the game, so that ere long they might have one of the best clubs in the county. The next toast was " The Ladies " given by the Presi- dent and responded to by Mr. Arthur Crosby. The President gave " Success to Runcorn Cricket Club," and Mr. G. S. Frith, captain, and Mr. James Green vice-captain responded. Mr. Frith said that matches for this summer had been arranged with Liver- pool Police, Winnington Park, Weston, Clubmoor, Birk- enhead Victoria, Widnes, Chester, Gateacre, and other clubs. The committee had secured another professional, and he thought the members would be satisfied with him. He was a first rate stumper, and had played for the Lancashire colts against the County, which was a very fair recommendation. Last season he was engaged with Rock Ferry. Mr. H. Wright proposed " The Veterans," coupled with the names of Messrs Gatley. and Woods, of whom he related some interesting reminiscences. The President gave "The Visitors'" coupled with the name of Dr. Gastaldi whose genial happy face they were happy to have amongst them. The toast was drunk with musical honours.—Dr. Gistaldi in responding expressed his determination to be present at their matches in future, and he would encourlge those people he influenced to take a deeper interest in the game, which tended to develop the body and to infuse grerter vital- ity into it. He had always admired the energy with ' which the English people entered ipto their sports,which • bethought played no small part in the formation of character. The other toasts were" The town and trade of Run- corn." responded by Mr. Cyril Chantler ; the health of the club's worthy friend and supporter Mr. J. R. Frith ; and the Host and Hostess (Mr. and Mrs. Dutton). The proceedings were enlivened by songs and instrumental selections and recitations given by Messrs. Nergle. Cyril Chantler, James Booth. and 11. Wright. Mr. C. Le Con- teur was the accompanist.

WIDNES WOMEN'S_ LIBE RAL

-ASSOCIATION.

The annual meeting of this association was held in the Liberal Club, Widnes, on Saturday night, Mrs. E. Stewart Brown, president, occupied the chair. The bon sec. Miss Deacon read the report which stated that since the formation of the association a year ago the membership had increased to nearly 700, most of whom lived in the borough of Widnes. The members had given valuable assistance during the County Council and the Parliamentary Elections to the Liberal candidates, and though in the Parliamentary election, the Liberal had not been successful, the large reduction of the majority of the Conservative member was no unimportant victory for the Liberal party in the division. In the course of the year various meetings had been held by the association, addressed by Miss Ryley, Mr. Shores, Mrs. E. Stewart Brown and Countess Alice Kearney, and the attendance of members showed that the meetings were greatly appreciated by the Liberal women of Widnes.—The officers were unanim- ously re-elected, viz: President, Mrs. E. Stewart Brown; hon. secretaries Miss S. and Miss H. Deacon ; hon. trea- surers, Mrs. O'Keeffe and Miss Morrison. The list of vice-presidents include Mrs. Gossage, Mrs. Timrnis, Mrs. E. K. Muspratt, Mrs. Deacon, Mrs. Henry Brunner.-- Mrs. Stewart Brown thanked the members for the honour they had conferred on her by electing her for al second time their president and briefly referred to the work of the association and also to the prospects of the Liberal party in the country. Amongst the many bene- ficial bills introduced by the Liberal Goverument, they, as women, noted with particular pleasure the Parish and District Councils Bill which provided for the election of women to these councils. At the conclasion of the busi- ness an interesting pnAgramme of music and recithtions was performed, in which Miss H. Deacon, Miss Carey, Mr. H. Wade Deacon and Mr. Morrison took part.

MESSRS. CROSFIELD'S EMPLOYES SOCIAL EVENINGS.

On Saturday evening, the last of the series of the social evenings promoted by the employes of Messrs. J. Crosfield and Sons, of Bank Quay Soap Works, was held in the Workmen's Mission Room. Mr. A. H. Crosfield occupied the chair. A feature of the concert was the appearance of the famous Mons. Rosetti as solo pianist, who played several selections in the highly accomplished manner. which has gainedhim celebrity. The " Campanello " (Liszt Paganini) was an unequivocal success in point of execu- tion, and the audience showed their appreciation by emphatic applause and a recall. Miss N. Roberts, and Miss Dalby, Mr. A. Griffith. Mr. J. Haynes, and Mr. W. Massey were the vocalists. "On the Doorstep" and " The Miller and the Maid " by Miss N. Roberts were in every way a success. Miss Dalby contributed" When the leaves begin to turn " and "I dreamed a dream." Mr. A. Griffiths received an encore for his fine rendition of "In '54," and in response gave " Johnny Sans." He also sang'' The Middy " which still further pleased the audience. Mr. J. Ilaynes, although indis- posed, sang " The Waiter" in good style and he recoived an encore. He also sang " The Missus " and " I've worked eight hours to-day." The comicality he infused in the pieces caused uproarious applause and laughter. Mr. W. Massey sang "The Boys of the Old Brigade," and "The Wolf," and received an encore for his rendi- tion of the latter. Mr. G. Whitfield gave two Lancashire recitations " The Boggart on'th Stump" and " The Respectable Mon" in an excellent manner. Miss A. Willet recited " Curfew must not ring:. to-night" and in response to loud applause she recited " The Little Pilgrim."—Mr. A. H. Crosfield, at the close of the con- cert said they had now brought to a close their series of social evenings for the season. He hoped the concerts would not la p e,but rather develop. There was,he said ,an old maxim which a professional gentleman present could endorse—" that it is a good thing to leave off with an appetite." Certainly musical culture might grow to wonderful results from small beginnings. So far as he could, he would be glad to get some of his friends to , assist them—(applause) ; but the thing was really in their own hands. He hoped they would give the cOa- certs their best support, and that they would have other and extended undertakings next winter and in subsequent years--k vote of thanks was passed to the Chairman, and to the ladies and gentlemen who had taken parts in the entertainments, on the proposition of Mr. Willet.

Two foreign pickpockets, one an American and the 'other a Spaniard acting as his confederate have each been sentenced at Nice by the Correctional 'Tribunal to five years' imprisonment. The accused had selected the arrival platform of the railway station as their field it operation, and committed numerous thefts. Many stolen articles were found in their posaessioa.

One of those facts is that during the last twenty or thirti years the propertied classes, and especially of the city of London, have been hostile to all reform. The trial of HOBBS, WRIGHT. The L rator and NEWMAM who were implicated ibe Frauds. in the Liberator frauds ended on Monday in the, under the circumstances, inevit- able verdict of guilty. Holm and WRIGIIT were sentenced to twelve years penal servitude each, and NEWMAN to five years. The frauds of which these men have been found guilty were, as Mr. JUSTICE HAWKINS said when sentencing them, of the cruellest description, inasmuch as they have resulted in depriving thousands of thrifty people of the whole, or the greater part, of the savings that had been accumulated during a life time. They had not, said Mr. JUSTICE HAWKINS, "paid any attention to the hundreds and thousands of homes they had rendered desolate by their robber- ies of the treasured savings of those who had entrusted their all to the Liberator." Even in regard to these criminals the plea under which unequal justice has been so often meted out was made. The sympathy of the court was asked because the prisoners had been living in affluence and comfort, and therefore punishment would be all the more felt by them. It would, surely, be a disgraceful travesty of justice if punishment were mitigated because malefactors had been enabled to live in affluence by means of the wealth of the victims they had swindled. As the judge remarked, whilst thinking of the prisoners he could not forget the people who in old age had been left beggars through their fadt, and the sentenees he awarded, whilst certainly not biassed by vindictiveness or panic, were exemplary, and should prove a deter- rent to evil-doers of a similar character. It is now said that the deficiencies in connection with the Liberator and its kindred societies amount to about seven millions of pounds. When account is taken of the fact that this immense sum is largely made up of the small contributions of compara- tively poor people, an idea may be formed of the wide-spread misery caused by this great financial collapse. Holm, who has been appropriately described as the " most conspicuous rascal of the group," was proved to have plundered the Libera- tor Society to the extent of two million pounds and even this did not cover the whole of his rogue- ries. Well might he live in " affluence and coin" fort," but it would, truly, have been a strange violation of the fitness of things if his punishment had been lessened because of this. As "3. W. HOBBS, Esq., J.P., of Norbury Hall," he was for some years a great man at Croydon. He held the office of Mayor, and was liberal in his benefactions, and even, it is said, had the honour of entertaining royalty at Norbury Hall. As a magistrate,he was "voluble," and was always as ready to give good ad- vice as to dispense justice. He wa s,we are informed particularly impressive in cautioning small boys, who were brought before him for apple-stealing that " these petty thefts lead to bigger ones." The eulogy which his missing friend, Mr. J. S. BAL- FOUR, passed upon him at the inaugural banquet of his Mayoralty is entertaining reading. "Few," said Mr. BALFOUR, " could bear testimony as he could to the sterling work which Mr. HOI3BS had done. Having followeel his career, he could bear testimony to the fact that his success in life, great as it had been, was owing to those sterling qualities—honesty, industry, and self-devotion— which, thank God, still always secured success when properly applied." It is just now a matter of general regret that the eulogist did not stand in the dock with the object of this eulogium, to receive thedue reward of his "honesty, industry, and self-devotion."

THE Yankees are a " smart" people and it will be interesting to watch the struggle that is about to take place amongst them against the dreaded crinoline.. The" plan of campaign" is ingenious, and in the devising of it human nature seems to have been taken into account The young men of America are, we are told, starting an anti-crino- line club. The rules of the club are of a strict and peremptory character. No young man who be- comes a member will be allowed to escort any lady wearing crinoline to a theatre, concert or dancing room, or, indeed, to any entertainment. No member will be permitted to call on a lady who receives her visitors in a hoop skirt, and at social gatherings he is to ruthlessly" boycot " the ladies who wear the crinoline. Jf he meet in the street any of his fair friends attired in the objectionable garb, he is to suddenly become interested in the overhead wires, or to be so absorbed in some abstruse mentul calculation as to be oblivious of all outward things. On the other hand, girls who foreswear the crinoline are to meet with the amplest reward. They are to be admitted as associates of the anti-crinoline club, and they are to be taken for drives, to the theatre, and, in short to receive all the little attentions which are denied to their recalcitrant sisters.

TARPORLRY HUNT. The annual Tarporley Hunt Steeplechase have been fixed for Wednesday. April 19th. The entries close on April 11th. Particularb will be found in our advertising columns. UMBRELLAS RECOVERED.—J. and W. DUTTON, Sankey-st. Warrington.—Advt. DEATH OF MR. J. UNSWORTH, OF CROFT. Mr. James Unsworth, sen., of Croft, died sud- denly at his residence at Lord-St., Croft, on Sunday at noon. Deceased was rather unwell on Saturday, but it was not thought necessary to call in a doctor. On Sunday morning begot up. but again feeling un- well he rested on the sofa. His son Joseph who lives with him, was in the kitchen, but, bearing a noise from his father, he ran to his assistance, and found him speechless, and he died a few minutes afterwards. Deceased was a warm supporter of St. Lewis's (R.C.) Chapel, Croft, and his loss will be deeply regretted by a large circle of relations and friends.

THE HOME IRON TRADE. With the near approach of the Easter holidays little change could be expected en the iron trade but on the whole, what alterations have taken place is in the direction of steadiness in prices of crude iron, the opinion becoming general that the bottom has been touched. In finished iron depression still prevails; but in tinplates inquiries still come to hand in fair numbers, although actual business transactions are limited. The steel trade is very quiet; but a slight movement is noticeable in the heavy branches in Sheffield. Engineers and ship- builders report no alteration. The coal trade j, per- haps, a trifle more active.—lron

JOSEPH PRITCHARP, Watchmaker and Jeweller, 61, Hick- et.. end 4, Bridge-st., Buncora.—Advt. GORED BY A COW AT RUNCORN. About ten o'clock on Monday, a cow was being driven to the slaughterhouse of Mr. Capper, butcher, Egerton- st., when on reaching Mill Brow the Animal became "mad," and rushed wildly along Halton-road. A boy was knocked down, but escaped uninjured. A number of people climbed walls and fences to escape the infuri- ated beast, but a young woman named Rachael New, of 20, Porter-st., was knocked down and gured. She received somewhat serious injuries and was confined to bed for several days. The animal was eventually cap- tured. CANAL MYSTERY AT PRESTON BROOK. At one o'clock on Wednesday afternoon the body of a female child was found floating in the Bridgewater Canal at Preston Brook. P.C. Griffiths had the body removed to the Red Lion Hotel, where an exansination revealed two large wounds on the head and a third on the left leg. It Is supposed that these have been in- ffcted by the screw of a passing steamer. The body is that of a child about two years of age, with brown hair, and the clothes consisting of a ragged petticoat and dress and a white chemise, with a piece sewn all round the bottom. Neither shoes nor stockings covered the infant's feet. Parrcaann's have now stood the test for 50 years, andquality and first-class workmanship are still guaranteed.-4clot GOOD FRIDAY AT RUNCORN AND WIDNES. The twin towns of the Mersey presented the appear- ance of Sunday, the tradesmen's shops being closed and services being held at the churches and several of the Nonconformist places of worship. Work was not en- tirely suspended in the chemical manufactories, there being such a press of orders that it was considered unwise to stop operations to the same extent as in former Tears. Runcorn railway station was closed as on Sun- days, and those who wished to leave the locality to visit friends or relatives were obliged to cross the bridge to Widnes, where the railway compahies offered the inducement of cheap fares to a number of places of inte- rest.

RUNCORN WATER BILL. When this bill came before Lord Morley (chairman of the committee), in its preliminary stages, there were present the Parliamentary agents, Mr. Frere for the Runcorn, Halton, and Western Waterworks Company, and Mr. Baxter for the Runcorn Improvement Commis- sioners, as well as Messrs. Wiswall, F. A. Lake, E. Mar- shall, and J. Ashton. Mr. Baxter asked that the clause forming the agreement for the sale and purchase of the works be struck outtand that one be substituted empow- ering the Commissioners to purchase by agreement or arbitration.—Mr. Frere strongly objected to such a course being adopted, and after arguments his Lordship declined to permit the alteration to be made. The Bill must come on as originally framed. Go to PRITCHARD'S for the best five guinea watch a work- ingman can have.—Advt. WIDNES FREE LIBRARY COMMITTEE. The monthly meeting was held on Wednesday evening in the Town Hall. M. H. Wade Deacon presided. The Librarian (Miss Proctor) reported that during February 12,932 persons visited the reading room, compared with 13,004 in the corresponding month last year. During the same period 5,160 works had been consulted, being a decrease of 320 in the lending department, and an increase of 25 in the reference department. The Libra- rian also presented the sixth annual report, which stated that during the past year the reading room had been open 308 days, and the visitors numbered 159,148. or a daily average of 517, being an increase on the previous year of 14 per day. 809 vols. 762 by purchase and 47 by gift, had been added to die library, making a total of 5,807 books now on theshelves. During the year,56,443 books had been issued for home reading, and 2,279 had been consulted in the reading rooms' which was an in- crease of 9 per day, compared with the previous year. The boys' reading room had been of the greatest service; as many as 63 boys had been counted in the room at the same time. The general reading room was much quieter lin consequence of the absence of the boys. The report was considered highly satisfactory, and was adopted.

Runcorn.—Ader. WOUNDING AN HOTEL MANAGER AT WIDNES. At Liverpool Assizes, on Tuesday, James Joseph Tobin, aged 27, and John Tobin, 24, labourers, were charged with having on the 17th December, in the Waterloo Hotel, lVidnes, wounded Owen Leneham, with intent to do him grevious bodily harm. Mr. Watson conducted the prosecution, and Mr. Ross Brown defended the pri- goner John Tobin, the other being undefended by court- sel. On the day named, the two prisoners were drink- ing in the hotel named, of which the prosecutor was manager. The prisoners were behaving in a disorderly manner, in consequence of which the prosecutor and his barmen found it necessary to eject them. In the strug- gle at the door the prisoner James Joseph kicked the pro- secutor who was struck with a brick by the other pri- soner and rendered unconscioul, serious injury being inflicted upon him. After evidence had been' given, his lordship ruled that there was no evidence against James Joseph of unlawful wounding. The jury found John guilty of wounding,and James Joseph guilty on a second count of having inflicted gravous bodily harm on the prosecutor by kicking him. His Lordship sentenced John to nine months' imprisonment, and the other prisoner to two months.

SOCIAL EVENING AT RUNCORN. -- LIBERAL CLUB. Another of the series of social evenings in con- nection with Runcorn Liberal Club, was held on Wednesday night, and about sixty members spent Several hours in a most enjoyable manner. Pro- ceedings commenced with a supper of meat pie, cake and coffee, provided by Mr. P. Speakman, J.P., C.C., and cigars were afterwards distributed also at Mr. Speakman's expense. A capital programme of songs and recitations was gone through by Messrs. Paton (Widnes), J. Hosker, J. Parker, J. Moss, E. Johnston, J. Stanway, and J. Bluadell. The comic songs and recitations of Mr. Paton were a special feature of the evening's amusement. Mr. Tom Lea presided, and the duties of accompanist were performed by Mr. Holt. On the proposition of Mr. John Bate seconded by Mr. H. Wright, and supported by Mr. R. Jones a cordial vote of thanks was accorded to Mr.Speakman for his genercsity. THE VICAR OF RUNCORN AND ST. MICHAEL'S CHURCH

In the April number of the " Parish Magazine " the Vicar writes as fellows with regard to St. Michael's and All Angels Church :—" It is-a source of very great satisfaction to me to be able to print the final balance sheet of the chancel of St. Michael's, showing that every liability has been discharged. The committee found themselves face to face with a deficiency of £125 ss. 6d., precisely, we considered the matter carefully and decided that too any appeals had already been made to the public to warrant on attempt- ing another. It only remained for us to dip into our own pockets, and each of us according to our ability I contributed his share with the result that our worthy I treasurer was able on Tuesday evening, March 21st, to I place his accounts and bank book before us settled up to the last halfpenny. These accounts were passed unanim- ously and, we dissolved ourselves. St. Michael's Building Committee has accordingly ceased to exist in any form, perhaps if Runcorn flourishes some day a new vicar and a new committee will build a tower and spire. I hope my successor will have a good head and will put on the weathercock himself."—The balance sheet shows a total expenditure of £1,676 N. 3d.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT

RUNCORN.

BY AN OCCASIONAL CORRESPONDENT.

Mr. Philip Speakman C.C. and Mr. Thomas Rigby, County Alderman of Sutton, have been placed on the commission of the peace. • We congratulate both gentlemen on the deserved honour which has been conferred upon them.

Considering the little excitement the Guardians' election aroused in Runcorn, it was somewhat sur- prising to find that sixteen candidates had been nominated for the seven seats. Mr. Philip Speak- man consequent upon his elevation to the magis- terial bench will be an ex-officio Guardian and his name therefore does not appear in the list. The other retiring members, Dr. Gastaldi, Messrs. Lea, Norman, and Taylor seek re-election. During the past year, they have attended regularly the meet- ings ot the committees and of the Board and have generally done good work for the ratepayers. There are rumours of many withdrawals and the number of candidates is certain to be greatly reduced before , the day of election arrives.

--- At the meeting of Runcorn Board of Guardians on Tuesday next the Board will be asked to adopt or otherwise the petition to the louse of Lords, asking for a reform of the Weaver Trust, by placing it under representative control, and giving the traders on the waterway more equitable terms. It was stated at the last meeting that Mr.Brunner's position in this matter was a selfish one ; at least that was the only construction the words used by Mr. Brandreth will bear, and he would not have uttered them had he given a moment's consideration to the matter. As a matter of fact the articles manufactured by Messrs.Brunner, Mond,and Co.,are conveyed on the Weaver at a considerably lower rate than salt, and Mr. Brunner has not only on one occasion but on many stated that it was unjust that salt should be taxed to the extent it is. lie wishes the tolls to be placed on a fair and equitable basis. The Runcorn Union is practically ruled by the farming community who enjoy a great prepon- derance on the Board, and as all men are more or less selfish, their opinions will be greatly influenced by the fact that amongst the exemptions from toll is manure intended for land contiguous to the river. But can any valid and sufficient reason be given why manure should be carried toll free at the expense of other traders? If manure be carried free, why not coals or any other goods required for trade purposes. The claim is quite as strong and the need just as great in one case as the other.

Mr. Brunner desires to place the Weaver under the control of a representative body consisting of delegates from the various local authorities border- ing on the river and that it shall be managed to benefit and not hamper trade as is the case at the present time. By what method of reasoning can it be shown that the trade of the Weaver should be taxed to reduce the county rate of townships that are not interested or connected in any way with the river. The area of benefit ought to be confined to the Weaver Valley. In the report of the Board of Trade, made in 1872, the following clause occurs: " In the case of the Liverpool town dues, which formerly fell on the whole trade of the Mersey, the , dues have been transferred to the dock estate and are now applied for the benefit of shipping, the Liverpool Corporation receiving, by way of com- pensation, a sum equivelant to about 10 years pur- chase of the dues. "By the Harbours and Passing Tolls, &c., Act, 1861, a number of other charges of a similar character were put an end to on somewhat similar conditions, viz., by allow- ing them to continue for ten years, and then extinguishing them. No reason can be alleged for treating the Weaver dues differently. The vested interest of a county differs in no res- pect from that of a town or other Corporation. The trust was from the commencement a public trust, and the £O,OOO advanced in the first instance by 14 Cheshire gentlemen, and afterwards repaid to them can no More be held to have given the county of Cheshire a right to impose a perpetual tax of £30,000 a year on the salt trade, than the trifling consideration paid two centuries since. by Liverpool to the Molyneux family for the Liverpool town dues, gave that town a right to a perpetual burden on the trade of the Mersey." If the Guardians con- sider the petition in a calm and judical manner there is only one conclusion at which they can arrive, and that is to support it.

PRESENTATION TO THE REV. J. W. WILCOCKSON OF WESTON POINT.

The worshippers of Christ Church, Weston Point, gathered in the schoolroom on Tuesday evening, to present their pastor, the Rev. J. 11. Wilcockson, and Mrs. Wilcockson, with a token of regard and esteem. The Rev, gentleman has been presented to the living of Henbury, near Macclesfield, by the Bishop of Chester, and 70 of the parishioners had subscribed to the cost of a hanasorne silver inkstand and a Morocco handbag. On the inkstand was inscribed " Presented to the Rev. and Mrs. Wilcockson, on their leaving Weston Point by the members of the congrega- tion of Christ Church, the scholars and teachers of the Sunday school and other friends as a token of esteem. March 1893-"--Mr. Rosenberg in making the presenta- tion, said the people while pleased that Mr. Wilcockson had been chosen the vicar of ffenbury, regretted to lose him. Be had been highly successful in all his parochial work, and the day and Sunday schools had progressed remarkably under his care. His name would also be always corinected with their reading room and coffee room, which were a monument to his perseverance and liberality. Mrs. Wilcockson had also been feremost in every good-work for the welfare of the parish.—ln ac- knowledging the gifts, the Rev. J. H. Wilcockson urged his hearers to give the utmost support to the day schools and pointed out that in the near future through. the increase of the population caused by the Ship Canal, the buildings would require to be enlarged. Churchmen ought to support their day schools because they were assured of the religious teach- ing given there, whereas in the Board School, they had no guarantee as to the religious instruction given. He alluded to the great improYement that had taken place in the attendance at Sunday school since he took charge of Christ Church, which was mainly due to the teachers and parents.. He hoped they would give his successor, the Rev. David Bowen from Hyde, a hearty welcome, and show him that he had their sympathy and were determined to assist him in his work. In conclusion he thanked them in the name of his mother, big wife and himself for the kind sentiments expressed towards them and for the handsome presents.—The proceedings then terminated.—The members of the Girls' Friendly Society have presented Mrs. Wilcockson with a duplex lamp with brass Corinthian columns.

BRITISH AND FOREIGN BIBLE SOCIETY. MEETING AT WIDNES.

The annual meeting of the Widnes Auxiliary was held in St. Paul's Parochial Room on Monday even- ing. Mr. B. Brown presided, and there was a fair attendance. A hymn having been sung and prayer offered by the Rev. T. J. Kent, the Rev. G. H. Gar- ret, said there was no formal report to read, but a very strong and representative committee had been formed to carry on the work of the Bible Society in the town. Every Protestant denomina- tion was represented on the committee and he was Pleased to think that they were so united in carry- ing out the great work of this Society. The officials appointed were: President, Rev. G. H. Garrett ; treasurer, Mr. F. Neil ; secretary, Mr. F. Ilawlinson ; committee, Revs. W. Stanton lones.R. Meiklejohn,Samp- son, T. J. Kent, R. P. Robertson, 0. Hughes, and R. G. Hughes ; Messrs. F. H. Pankhurst. A. Tracey, A. Borth- wick, D. J. Brookes, G. Stone, H. Johnson, S. Kidd, C. Davies, J. Newborn, J. Robinson, S. Owens, D. Lewis, W. Hodgkinson, and R. Roberts.—The Chairman said he was pleased to think that although there were differ- ences between the Protestant deoominations, they could at least stand on the same platform when the Bible was concerned. He trusted it would be their endeavour to enable the Bible Society to continue in its good work in the future as it had done in the past, in extending the kingdom of Christ. Englishmen owed a great deal to this Society, but there were some societies which did not seem to obtain much sympathy from the general put> lic. He did not think the English public grasped the vast importance and influence of the Bible Society. If one quarter of the money spent in races and football were given to the Society it would not be so much in debt. The Society had an annual income of £114,000, and there was a deficiency of £23,000. It was time that they in Widnes gave increased help to the Society,instead of sending £4 they ought at least to coutribute £2O. (Applause.)—The Rev.C.F.Moss, of Southport, formerly missionary at Madagascar delivered an interesting lec- ture on "The Bible in Madagascar." The lecture was admirably illustrated by lantern views shown by the Rev. R. Meiklejohn.—On the motion of the Rev. G. A. Le Bert seconded by the Rev. R. G. Hughes, a vote of thanks was accorded to the chairman and lecturer, and a similar compliment was paid to the Vicar for the use of the room, on the motion of Mr. S. Owe..s seconded by Mr. F. Laurinson. The collection realised £2 10s., as comnared with 19s. last year.

WIDNES TOWNSIIIP MEETING.

The annual meeting of the ratepayers of the town- ship of Widnes, was held in the Overseer's Office, Victoria-road on Thursday evening. There were present Messrs. F. Neil (in the chair), D. Wright, F. H. Pankhurst, It. Whiteside, E. Al artl ey. T. S. Swale, T. Atherton, 1). J. Brookes, Joynsou, J. Midwood, J. Goff (assistant overseer). The Chairman in the course of the usual financial statement said they commenced the year with a balance of £513 10s. sd. and they received from the rate laid in MO £14,156 13s. 11d., which with interest amounting te £23 Os. Id., brJught up the total receipts to £14,693 4s. sd. There had been paid to Present Union and county calls £10,600 ; Widnes School Board, £3,500 ;other pur- poses, £204 12s. 6d. ; leaving a balance in hand of £3BB lie, lid. The Chairman explained that the School Board precept was £4,500, but the overseer had a balance of a thousand pounds which they paid to the School Board on the 25th March. Owing to the incor- poration of the town the collection of some monies, which had previously been in the hands of the Local Board would now be collected by the overseers. A pre- cept had been served upon the overseers by the Council for £10,552 6s. 9d., lr a rate of Is. 7d. in the Z. Sop- posing that the amount required by the Union would be same as last year £9,000, the total amount required was £19,752 6s. gid. which would mean a rate of 3s. in the £. Last year the rate was 2s. in the £. The precept from the Town Council included the School Board precept of £5,000 and also the police. A dismission took place during which the Chairman stated that the poor rate and district rate last year was 4s. 6d. He was certain that amount would be exceeded this year. There were many items which would not I occur again. There was the cost of incorporation,a very large item. They might anticipate a reduction in the district rate, from the fact that expenses of the Library, the Technical Schools, the Town Clerk's Department, the Town Hall expenses, a portion of the salary of tho Sur- veyor and other officials were included in the la3rough rate precept which the overseers had to collect. These items had been transferred from the district rate to the poor rate, and as they collected the latter on the full as- sessment from railways and agricultural land, whereas before they only paid on one-fourth, this was to the advantage of the general body of ratepayers. The following gentlemen were nominated for the office of overseers :—Messrs. F. Neil, D. Wright, Joshua Mid- wood, F. H. Pankhurst, Joseph Robinson, and T. Ather- ton.. Regret was expressed that Mr. James White de- clined to again accept office.—A vote of thanks was passed to the assistant overseer, whose ability and atten- tion to duty was spoken of in high terms of praise.

AT OUR LOCAL CEURCHES.

THE RECTOR ON THE WESLEYAN& NOT THE LAST ADDRESS. The Rector (the Rev. F. W. Willis), at the Parish Church on Sunday night, in an address upon "The Wesleyans," said that he had come to the last of the addresses of which he had given notice, but he proposed to have one concluding address the following Sunday, upon "The Holy Catholic Church." He would like to say how glad he was to have seen such large attendances, and he would also wish to say what he did not care to say by writing to the papers, how intensely grieved he was, if by these addresses, he had stirred up any unkind feeling. He had no intention of doing auy- thing of the kind. fie had no idea of the matter becom- ing so public, although he had no objection whatever to it. The last form of dissent, which he would discuss that night, was the Wesleyans, who separated from the Church on doctrinal grounds in the last century. The 18th century was a time of great stagnation, and it was then that the Unitarians appealed to the upper classes, while the Wesleyans by their earnestness appealed to the misses. His address that night would be divided into four points—first, the history of Wesleyanisrn ; second, what he conceived to be their errors ; third, their excel- lencies; and, fourth, causes for regret and thankfulness. Wesley was born in 1703, and died in 1791, his life thus almost extending the whole length of the century. His grandfather was one of the Presbyterian ministers who were ejected in 1662,aud hie father was a clergyman and incumbent of Ep worth in Lincolnshire. He was the Seeond of three eons, Samuel, John, and Charles. John was sent to in Charterhouse, and afterwards gained a fellowship n Lincoln College. Ile was ordained a dea- con in 1725. He joined a band of most earnest young men, and lived a very holy life. Ia 1735, he went to America on a mission to the Indians and settlers of Georgia: On the voyage he fell in with some Moravians i who believed n sudden, sensible conversion, instantane- ous perfection, and after attaining that perfection taught an inability to sin. Wesley adopted their doc- trines and gives the time of his conversion at 9 o'clock, on Wednesday, May 24th, 1738, and said that he had never been a Christian before then. This influence of the Moravians was only temporary. In 1739 he fell under the influence of the French Convulsionists who were refugees from the continent. These fell into a kind of religious hysteria under the influence of addresses given by their preachers. In 1744, Wesley and Whitfield separated, and from the latter arose the Calvinistic Methodists, known as Lady Huntingdon's sect and in the same year the first Wesleyan Conference Was held. In 1750, the idea was first broached of separation from the church, and in 1784 Wesleyanism was established. A deed of declaretien was drawn up and enrolled in Chancery, giving unlimited powers to the legal hundred preachers of the conference, and trusting it was all the property,and power to settle all questions by a majority of votes. The three years' system was also settled then. In this year Wesley, although he was only a priest, ani not a bishop, ordained two laymen as presbyters and consecrated two clergymen as bishops, from which arose the Methodist Episcopalian Church of America. Just be- fore Wesley died, he wrrote a solemn warning to his fol- lowers not to separate from the Church of England, but in 1795 the actual separation took place, and from that time to 1849, there were several divisions from them. One of their errors was the confusion of regeneration and conversion. Regeneration was a change out of the world into the church, and was shown by baptism, which showed their change out of the family of the world to the familyof God. Conversion meant a change of heart and life, and could only take place in an adult. There must be two persons in conversion, the Holy Spirit couvincing„ and the person yielding to His im- pulses. Regeneration could and did take place at bap- tisin,and evervlittle infant received regeneration,although its heart was not touched. Their second great error was that conversion was entrance into the church. By baptism they entered the Church,and not be conversion. There were good and bad children in the Church as in a family. The Wesleyans also believed that conversion was necessery for all. Of course there were a great many who needed it, but it was not necessary for all. There were souse who never knew a time when they did not love and serve God. There were others, who like Wesley, could nssne the very day and hour and place when the blessed change took place. It was so with St. Paul and St. Augustine, but yet, it was not absolutely necessary for all. Their fourth error was a belief hi instantaneous perfection. The Church believed in a gradual growing in grace, but the Wesleyans believed in an instantaneous perfection and thence an inability to sin. This however, was qualified by saying" So long as God keeps us." Among their excellencies was the revival of spirituality. At the time of the church's slumber in the last century, Wesley's soul was on fire to win souls for God, and the Wesleyans are still deeply spiritual men, and he (the Rector) could not speak too highly of their work among the masses. " They con- descend to men of low estate." They work amongst the poor and the fallen. They have also a wonderful organi- setion, with their circuits, ministers, preachers, and classes, hut their organisatiort was not to be c.impared with the Church in its dioceses,bishops, parishes, priests and as,istant curates. One great cause of regret was their leaving of the Church of England, although they fcelowed a man who was an incumbent of the English Church, and who implored them not to leave it. Another cause of regret was the exercise of the ministerial func- tion by men not qualified to do it. Only bishops could ordain, and only priests could celebrate holy communion. A cause for thankfulness, however, was their earnestness and another cause or thankfulness was their friendly attitude toward the Church. Some b -dies had become very political and antagonistic toward the Ciiurch. He did not think that was so with the Wesleyans. :There were dissenters and dissenters, and it was a cause of thankfulness that the Wesleyans were so friendly. He believed there were still some Wesleyans who had their children baptised in the Church, and were also married and buried by church clergymen. THE P.S.A. AT WYCLIFFE. On Sunday, the usual pleasant Sunday afternoon ser- vice was held in Wycliffe Church. There was again a large attendance. The chair was occupied by the Rev. J. W. Varley, whe delivered a brief address. Miss N. Roberts contributed a solo. An address was delivered by Dr. Waite of Liverpool, which he preceded by the singing of the solo " Rock of Ages " in an excellent manner. Mr. Thomas Wallineton, secretary, said that the membership had now reached 430. He wished to correct an error which had gained currency in the town that none were wanted at the services unless they be- came members of the society. He wished it to be un- derstood that all men were welcome to attend the ser- vices whether they enrolled themselves as members or not. On Monday evening a meeting of the members of the P.S.A. at Wycliffe was held in the schoolroom, Bewsey- street, for the purpose of electing officers and a com- mittee. The Rev. J. Yonge was elected president ; Mr. Thomas Wallington, secretary ; Mr. A. Knowles, regis- tration secretary ; Mr. Atkinson, treasure! ; and a committee of twenty members was also appointed. How SMALLPDX IS SPREAD. At the last meeting of the St. Helens Health Commit- tee, Dr. Robertson reported four new cases of smallpox, ono of which was a man who had been to Wrexham. It has since transpired that the man went to a house in vvreoham where four persons had taken smallpox, of whom two, unvaccinated, had died,while two who were vaccinated in childhood, recovered. The man had at- tended the funeral of one of the victims, and brought the germs away with his clothes. Another case has arisen in St. Helens through contact with this marl, and other cases may follow.

!THINGS HEARD AND SEEN

AT WARRINGTON.

"Going in" for a thing is a common collo- qualism which indicates one action as a rule; but Geo. Bate who is an itinerant solicitor of alms has exercised the ingenuity which characterizes his race to tlarowi ng a plurality of meaning into the phrase "going in" for anything. Last Saturday he became aware of the approach of the Easter holidays, and wishing to compare favourably for once amongst his fellow beings, he decided to "go in" for a pair of new shoes. So he went to the Boot Company's shop in Bridge- street, and after consulting his fastidious taste, and studying the whereabouts of the shop- man, he selected a pair of" hobnails," and strolled into Mersey-street. lie threw his old " pushers" generously at a dog and sat down to try on his new Owes. By and by D.S. Monoghan came and said he was looking for a pair of new shoes. So he took the boots off George, and to prevent him hurrying out of town with the notion that War- rington people were ungenerous, Monoghan in- vited him to the mansion in Buttermarket-street, and on Monday, after a formal interview with a Bench, he succeeded—after going in" for new boots, in 'going in" for a month.

There should be some interest felt in Warrington in the industrial training ship " Clio" for this town contributes a fair proportion of intractable young- sters to " man " the vessel ; and, besides, has not the Corporation lately increased its modest sub- scription in support of the vessel? I notice that Mr. Henry Rogers, 1131.1.,has just made the official inspection of the ship, which lies in the Menai Straits, and he records continuous progress of a satisfactory character, a good tone among the boys, land excellent order and discipline. The ship, we are told, was clean, and the organisation excellent,the staff of officers working harmoniously together for the improvement and the instruction of the neg- lected children under their care. Good training in nautical instruction is given under competent sea- men, and also tailoring, shoemaking, sailmakiug, and carpentry. He examined the classes and found 1 their education thoroughly attended to. The boys were in good spirits, and seemed happy and cor?- fortable. One boy was seriously ill, and four or five ailing. Conduct had been on the whole satisfac- tory. There were 266 boys on board.

Th• captain superintendent, W. Milner Moger, R.N., reports that over 1,300 boys have been entered on the ship books since the " Clio" was established as a training ship in 1877. Over 1,000 have been discharged, and 84 per cent, were doing well,mostly at sea. Over 200 are R.N.R. men.

Last week I had the temerity to deprecate the growing popularity of the "cancerous weed," amongst Warrington young women. I was sur- prised at the number of iady friends whom this disparagement seemed to annoy. This week, how- ever, lam going to show them that the crusade against smoking does not merely effect females. Our American cousins have recently been attempt- ing to get a measure passed preventing a youth,who is unfortunate enough to be under 14, from squandering his pocket money upon, and destroy- ing his health by, the use of tobacco. What a source of revenue they would do away with in England if it were carried out. But our Yankee, friends go still further. The Washington Legisla- ture has passed a Bill making the sale of cigarettes illegal, in the state of Washington.

When I read this I said : I will tell my anti-to- bacconist friends andinduce them to persuade their " subjects " to emigrate to moral Washington. Then I read a little more and the paper said " But the motive of the Legislature is neither ethical nor sanitary, but economical. Nine-tenths of the mem- bers who voted for the Bill did not care a nickel about the reform of the cigarette fiend, but they were anxious to knock out the Tobacco Trust,which made profits last year out of cigarettes of four , million dollars."

Possibly one of the worst features of Warrington, and one which the Corporation are finding the most difficulty in dealing with, is that of the "slum " or insanitary property which the Medical Officer of Hoalth has at various times condemned as" unfit for human habitation." The Salford Corporation are I notice, coping with the same problem. They have purchased a considerable amount of slum property, and are abont to spend £lO,OOO in erecting " model dwellings" for the poor. The dwellings will be in flats and each tenement will contain a living room, bedroom. scullery and we. There is not much doubt of the success of such a scheme if it were carried out in Warrington.

Good Friday passed off in Warrington in a quiet kind of way, but although there was an absence of wilful " contravening of the law," I have an idea that if a certain judge's definition of a bona fide traveller were acted upon this locality there would be quite a number—probably a few hundreds—of thirsty souls before the magistrates next week. The judge in question said that a man had no right to refreshment on licensed premises during prohibited hours unless he could prove that he had travelled the "limit " on legitimate business ! The only business done by some of those who had refreshments yesterday would be the purchase of intoxicating liquors. And somehow I don't think that would be legitimate business! MERLIN.

PRESENTATION TO INSPECTOR CLARK

SON OF WIDNES.

Art interesting ceremony took place in Widnes Police Court on Wednesday afternoon. when In- spector Clarkson, of Horwich, near Bolton, formerly sergeant of police at Widnes, was the recipient of a handsome black marble timepiece and a valuable keyless gold watch from the tradesmen of the town as a token of respect and esteem. In the absence of Mr. H. Wade Deacon who was unable to fulfil his promise to make the presentation, Supt, Keighley ful- filled the duty. In handing the gifts to Inseector Clarkson, the Superintendent said they testified to good feeling which existed between the public and the police in Widnes, which he hoped would ever cemtinue. That was the second occasion upon which he had acted as chairman at presentations to police officers in Widnes. He hoped Inspector Clarkson would long live to look at the presents and have them handed down to members of his family to remind them of the Widnes people. Dur- ing the time he, (Superintendent Keighley) had been in Widnes he had found Mr.Clarkson most humane to those with whom he had to deal. He had also often given ad- vice to those who needed it, and in many cases brought them back to the paths in which they ought to tread. It was the duty of police officers to do that, although it was rather difficult to carry it out at times, but they had to deal with people sometimes who would have gOne very much further wrong, but for the kifid advice given them by the police. (Applause.) Nothing gave him greater pleasure than to hand the testimonials over to Inspector Clarkson. (Applause.) Inspector Clarkson, in reply, said he could hardly find words that would sufficiently thank them for the con- sideration they had shown towards hini,not only on that occasion, but during the twenty-one years he had been in Widnes. He felt he was not deserving of the high character *Superintendent Beighley had given him, but ha had always tried to carry out the duties of his office faithfully and conscientiously,knd apparently he had done so satisfactorily, or they would not be honouring him with their presence that day. (Applause.) He well re- membered with what feelings he came to Widnes twenty- two years ago, for he was told he would be poisoned with chemical gas, and that a policeman's life was of no value in the town ; but he had tound that the more he knew of the place and the people the better he liked them. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES

A WARNING TO BUTCHERS. -John Kelly, Stoney-yard. butchers assistant, was charged at the Warrington Borough Court on Monday, with furiously driving a horse and spring cart along Horsemarket-st., on March 17th. P.C. Evans said that on the 17th inst., he saw defendant driving a butcher's vehicle down Horsemarket-St., at a fast rate. He knocked over a man named Newton, and drove off with out stopping. Mr. Newton corroborated the officer's statement, and defendant was fined 10s. and costs, Mr. Monks saying the butchers made a practice of driving fast, but if any more were brought up on a similar offence they would be more severely dealt witia. ALLEGED CRUELTY TO A HORSE. John Bradbury was charged at the Warrington Borough Court, on ihursday, with ill-treating a horse,on March 20, by working it while in an urtfit state, Inspector Dowty, R.S.P.C.A., said that from information received from P.S. Smith, he went on the 21st inst. to see defendant's horse at the Horse and Jockey stables. He found it to be very lame in the fore legs and unfit for work. He had stopped defendant for the same horse previously. P.S. Smith also gave evidence. Defendant contended that the horse examined by Inspector Dowty was not the same horse for which his son was stopped by P.S. Sinith, and that there was a discrepancy be- tween the descriptions of the horse and also its height in the evidence of the witnesses. The magistrates ad- journed the case for a proper examination to be made. AN AFTER BREARFAST THIEF. John Austin was charged on Monday, at the Warring- ton Borough Court, with stealing from the yard of the Warrington Slate Company, Arpley-road, on Saturday 25th inst., a man's pilot jcket,two pockethandkerchiefs and an ounce of tobacco, valued 10s. John Lowe, labourer for the company, said that he left his coat after breakfast near the Buxton Lime Office on Saturday, and when he went for it at eleven o'clock, it was gone. The tobacco and handkerchiefs were in the jacket pockets. Prisoner had been about the yard that morning. Patrick Daly. son of the lodging-house keeper, Scotland-road, said that he received the jacket from prisoner, to be put away for him.—Detective Monaghan said that he receiv- ed information from Lowe, and arrested prisoner in Winwick-st. He was drunk at the time. The coat was obtained from Daly's lodging-house, and was identified by Lowe. Prisoner was sent to gaol for a months hard labour. -

A 6` TOMMY ATKINS "IN TROUBLE. At the Borough Court on Wednesday, James Henry Ward, a soldier from Orford Barracks, was charged with breaking into the house ef Patrick Barclay, 11. Morris'- yard, Silver-square, with intent to commit a felony, about one o'clock on Wednesday morning. From the evidence it appeared that the prisoner had effected an entrance by pushing open the lower half of the kitchen window, and when found by the occupants of the house he was standing before the fire. The prisoner was not drunk, nor had he stolen anything. With difficulty he was brought to the police station by Patrick Barclay and the lodger Samuel Bennett, and P. C. Clough. The pris- oner slid he had been brought there by a young woman. A sergeant from the barracks said prisoner had been in the army eleven years and bore a very good character. The case was dismissed, on the prisoner promising to pay the two witnesses their wages for lost time. IN FOR CHEAP SHODDING. George Bate was charged on Saturday, at the War- •rington Police Court,with stealing from the People's Boot Co., Bridge-st., m the 24th inst., a pair of boots, valued ss. lid. The Chief Constable said that prisoner was a labourer lodging in Percival's-fold. The boots were seen safe at the door of the shop at 7-30 on the 24th inst., but a quarter-of-an-hour afterwards they were missing. Joseph Beckall, manager of the People's Boot Co., re- ported the matter to Detective Monaghan, also showing him a specimen boot. Detective Monaghan at once visited the various pawnshops and public-houses near, and at length reached the Leigh Arms, in Mersey-street, where he saw prisoner lacing a pair of boots. Seeing that they were new boots, Monaghan examined them, and found that they resembled the description of the boots missing. When questioned, prisoner said that he bought them from Scales and Salter's. Detective Monag- han took him to the People's Boot Co., and the boots were at once identified. Prisoner was sent to gaol for a month's hard labour. INHARMONIOUS MATRIMONY. William Dumbell, jun.,was charged at the Warrington Police Court, on Saturday, with unlawfully leaving his wife and children chargeable to the Warrington Union. on March 23. Mr. Shearer,who appeared for the Ur said that defendant's wife applied for admission to the house on the 15th inst., for herself and three children. Witness visited her house on the 17th inst., and they were admitted on the 23rd inst. The husband,who had had a separatiomorder made against him twelve months ago, and ordered to pay lls, a week to his wife,but who had only paid it for three months, was written to, and told to appear before the Board of Guardians. He,how- ever, came too late, and the Guardians ordered him to be prosecuted. Mr. Fairhurst, defendant's father-in- law, gave evidence as to defendant failing to pay to- wards the support of his wife. Mr.Davies,who appeared for defendant, said his client was willing to live with his wife again, but couIIII not get her to come to him. Mr. Brandreth spoke as to defendant's good character, and Mr. Shearer also stated that the wife appeared to be willing to live with her husband again. The case was adjourned for 14 days to see if the parties could come to satisfactory terms.

--- - - - HEARTLESS ROBBERY. At the Borough Court, on Tuesday, James Taylor Was charged with stealing, on Saturday., a leather purse. containing 3s. 3d., the property of Elizabeth Wrigley, 13,Edward-St. Detective Monaghan said that complain- ant and prisoner used to lodge at the same house. On Saturday, prisoner came to complainant's to see a child that was dead. Mrs. Wrigley had collected the money to bury- the child, and it was in a purse on the table. She went out to a neighbour's house, and when she re- turned prisoner was gone and had taken the money. He was wearing some clothing which he had exchanged with a soldier, and afterwards changed them again With the soldier with the result that when Mrs.VVrigley went to the barracks to identify him,she could not find him. Wit- ness arrested prisoner in Bruchemite's lodging-house with the purse in his possession. The child had not been buried up to Tuesday, as Mrs. Wrigley could not pay for the funeral owing to the theft. The magistrates thought this te be a particularly bad case, and as heartless as it .could possibly be, and sent prisoner to gaol for three -months' hard labour.

TRANMERE WANDERERS.—The above match took place at Birkenhead, Warrington win- ning the toss elected to play down-hill. Half-time score :—Warrington,3 tries and 3 minors to nil. Warrington re-started. A good bit of work by the home blacks carried play to the visitors' lines, but Hamblett waste the front by a splendid run and kick. Rowe passed to Smith, and that player neatly turned it over to Mannion, and he to Gibson—a splendid piece of play, ending in the latter being pulled up on the line. Rowe and Mannion had hard' lines in not dropping goals. Eventually Thornton scored, and the kick again being a failure, a fast and pleasant game ended in a victory for the visitors. WARRINGTON JUNIOR LEAGUE.

PART'SH CIITJRCH V. LATCRFORD.—The first meeting of these clubs took place on Saturday, on the ground of the formei 'rhe homesters had the same team (with the exception that Gallop occupied Frith's place) that de- feated St. Mary's on Monday evening, and Latchford had also mustered strongly. The opening exchanges were very even, but by degrees the visitors got to the home line, only to see Grarrity break away in fine style, and a bri'liant display between the same player, Carey, and Welsby,saw the latter scoring far out. Lawton took the kick and landed a grand goal, a success which was greeted with loud, cheers by the home team's supporters. From now to half-time the Churchites had a little the best of the argument, but ends were changed with no addition to the score. The second half was evenly-con- tested. Daniels got over after a smart piece of work, whilst Thornton scored for the visitora. Both full-backs (Bolesworth and Morgan) were applauded for their bril- liant play, and at the call of time the Parish Church retired winners by one goal and one try to one try, or seven points to two. The visitors held a slight advan- tage forward, but this was more than counterbalanced by the grand combination of the home backs. It would be unfair to mention any individual in particular, for all the team played well. On behalf of Latchford, Morgan, Hughes, and Dakin worked hard and well. Referee,Mr. J. Boyle.

THE SMALLPDX EPIDEMIC AT WARRINGTON.

In the House of Commons on Tuesday evening, Mr. Pierpoint asked the Under-Secretary to the Local Gov- ernment Board whether he had received any reports from the Medical Officer of Health for Warrington, stating the number of eases of smallpox which occurred during the late epidemic in Warrington, and giving the numbers of cases and deaths amongst properly vaccinated persons compared with those amongst non-vaccinated or imperfectly vaccinated persons and whether he would lay such reports upon the table. Sir Walter Foster said the Local Government Board had not yet received the reports of the Medical Officer of Health for Warrington as to the number of cases of smallpox which occurred during the late epidemic, and giving the number of cases among properly vaccinated persons compared with those among non-vaccinated or imperfectly-vaccinated persons. As he had previously stated, the Royal Commission on Vaccination was caus- ing an inquiry to be made as to the recent epidemic at Warrington. Mr. Pierpoint asked when the hon. gentleman expected to get the reports. Sir Walter Foster said he could not give any definite date, but probably in two or three months.

COLLAPSE OF PARR. OLD HALL, ST. HELENS.

NARROW ESCAPE OF EIGHT PERSONS. Early on Saturday a orning a portion of the Old Hall, at Parr, St. Helens, which was erected in the 17th century, and was the oldest inhabited house in the district, collapsed, and Isaac Pope, a boiler maker, and Margaret, his wife, their six children, and John Gandy, a carpenter, who also lived in the house, had a narrow escape of being buried in the ruins. " Baine's History of Lancashire " states that early in the 16th century the estate belonged to Sir Thomas Parr, who was Master of the Wards and Comptroller to Henry VIII., and whose daugh- ter Catherine Parr became Queen to that Monarch. Tradition says that this Queen was born here, but history only goes to show that she was educated with her mother at Kendal Castle, where Sir Thomas had gone to assist in opposing the Scottish invasion. Be this as it may, the Old Parr Hall has an iateresting his- tory. From the Parr family it went into the Orrell family, and has been used as a mansion, grange, farm- house, schoolhouse, and for the last thirty years as tene- ments. The hall has been sub-divided into four houqes, one of which was occupied by Pope and his family. On Friday night they retired as usual, but about two o'clock a portion of the roof fell with a thundering noise. Pope and his wife and youngast child were sleeping down- stairs. Three boys were sleeping in the room over their heads, and John Gandy and another boy were in the great bedroom in the centre of the block. It was the roof of this latter room which had given way. Pope rushed upstairs to find his boy and Gandy uninjured. although the romn was wrecked. The inmates were all got out safely, and while the furniture was being re- moved another fall occurred, and the roof, front wall and windows were carried bodily into the cultivated field adjoining. Much damage was done. An inspec- tion of the place indicated that some of the old beams in roof had become rotten and honeycoro bed, and although the building had been tied up with iron rods they were insufficient. The portion in question ha tl recently un- dergone some repairs, the outer walls having been re- pointed. The PA rr :lan estate is now the property of the Rev. Father J. J. Lennan of Weld Bank, Chorley.

THE LOCAL VETO BILL. _- MR. GLADSTONE ON VESTED INTERESTS.

The Secretary of the Liberal and Radical Anti-Sunday Closing Union lately wrote to Mr. Gladstone, enclosing a copy of a resolution passed at a meeting of Liberal and Radical working-men, condemning certain clauses in the Liquor Traffic (Local Control) Bill, and urging the right hon. gentleman to use his influence with the Govern- mentlo induce them to re-consider their position respect- ing that measure. Mr. Gladstone has replied as fol- lows :

10, Downing-st., Whitehall. 28th March, 1893.

Sir,—l am desired by Mr. Gladstone to acknowledge the receipt of your letter of the 28th inst., with the ac- companying resolution, which shall receive due con- sideration on the part of the Government. With respect to the quotation contained in the latter part of your letter, Mr. Gladstone thinks that since the decision of the courts in the case of Sharpe v. Wakefield, few persons will con- tend that the license-holder has any interest in his license which can properly be described as a vested interest. He may still, however, have a claim to equitable con- sideration, and this the bill aims at giving him. Whether it is rightly given in favour, form, and amount Parliament will no doubt consider caref GEO. W. MURRAY. KAN'S COMPOUND Essence of Linseed, Aniseed, Senega, Squill, Tolu, &e., 90. 130., &C. SIX IN A PUNT.

A GOOD FRIDAY ON THE MERSEY.

BT LUNE BLUNT. In accordance with a mutual agreement Mc.illester had consented to awaken me at 6 a.m. on Good Friday morning. So at 7 a.m. punctually, he came and threw cinders at my bedroom window, and with characteristic activity,l was up and dress- ing by 7.30. I soon had breakfast, and having packed up two potato pies, a custard, some potted lobster, a novel, two' spoons, some tea, two comic papers, and some sugar, all in a basket, we started to meet the other four, at the boathouse near Wilderspool Causeway. They had promised to be there by eight, at the appointed rendezvous, and sure enough, when we got there, we didn't see them waiting impatiently for us. By half-past eight, however, they had all straggled up with eyes bulged out with recent sleep, and pockets bulged with provisions. It was a big four oared boat or the punt, we had commissioned, and we intended to row as far as Runcorn and come back on the 21 foot tide. It is the manner in which we so in- geniously didn't do this that will always fix that voyage on my mind as an instance of the sad and disastrous consequences which follow in the wake of a party of boatmen who can't row . Ah How sad those results were when I think of them ; re- sults which severed six friendships ; which turned the regard of six young men for each other from warm, tropical trusting sunbeams of fraternity, to chilly, silent, icebergs.

None of us could row ; but we were all attired in borrowed boating flannels with the exception of Gilbertson ; he wore a frock coat and a silk hat. We were pushed off, and then commenced the most colossal forgery upon four- oared rowing you could imagine. The oars splashed into the water some seconds after each other; spray was scattered over the subscriber,who was steering; on the sixth stroke Gouldton's oar slipped out of the rowlock and he fell on his back in the bottom of the boat ; and at the eighth stroke AlcAllester "caught a crab," and lost his oar. It took five minutes floundering about before the lost imple- ment was regained. Then McAllester, who is a powerful and stubborn Scot, began to pull his stroke oar in a most furious manner, at the rate of about 30 strokes to the minute. So that by the time we got opposite the brewery we succeeded in running aground on the bank. We started again; the morning was bright and fresh ; the sun had hung his golden rays on the budding tree boughs and spread them like a golden gauze upon the green grass of the meadows ; and the larks were piping cheerily away far up in the blue sky. So we all felt friendly and didn't do any- thing more than laugh at our own ramshakling way of getting the boat along. But AlcAllester seemed to have become imbued with the idea that he was going to row that craft himself, and so he kept up his furious style. For a minute or two his oar would go "Splash ! splash ! splutter-te-splash ! splash! splash !" in quick stliccession, and then he'd pull us right into the bank. No matter how we told him to "ease off Mac" he'd just have the boat try- ing to climb the bank on his side, every time. This was becoming so frequent that we got to telling him not to commence rowing, until the other three had pulled the boat to the opposite side of the ' river, so as to give them a chance. And he waited quietly until we got to the other side, and then he'd wade into that rowing again, and have us on the other side of the river before you could say "cooked clams" three times. By-and-by this became mono- tonous ; it was a matter of the three men pulling against the Scot and the boat making little head- way. Everytime we struck the bank on McAlles- ter's side, we didn't laugh any more, but swabbed perspiration from our faces and said things to our- selves that don't appear in Sunday School books. Presently ominous mutterings and grumblings were thrown at the sturdy stroke, and when he flared up indignantly and said he was doing all the work in the boat, there was a prospect of a mutiny. But by an ingenious scheme of mine we managed to regain our former tranquility, although the enthus- ism was decreasing as the Begs grew on the oara- mens' hands. The scheme was that the other man who rowed on McAllester's side should "back water" against him, whilst the other two pulled against him. This balanced matters nicely and we didn't run on the bank more than once, or may-be twice, in a couple of hundred yards.

in this manner we reached and passed the " ratch "opposite Monks, Hall and Cos. works, and then the difficulty of following the winding channel commenced. Three times we grounded on barely concealed sand banks; and three times we succeeded in getting back to the channel. But when the fourth time came we had struck a bank 12 feet wide, and covered with a foot of water. The channel ran the other side of it so we decided to get out and wade over the bank with the boat. All took off their shoes and stockings and in addi- tion Gilbertson took off his "condenser." Gilbert- son was pulling a rope at the bow; and conse- quently when we heard a loud splash, and saw the fancy " Gilly " glide away into the deep water, we knew that the rope must have broken. Indeed we knew it had, because it had gone with Gilbertson. ! In a moment we were in the boat and after him, McAllester doing the only redeeming thing of the voyage by dragging him out by the hair. The difficulty was now: How was our friend to get through the day in a suit of wet clothes. He wanted us to row him back home, but the matter was voted upon and all except Gilbertson were in favour of his pulling his clothes off and letting them dry in the boat. He pulled them off and spread them about. By and by he said he felt cold with nothing on but his dry silk hat; so we hauled out a sack and cut two lwles in the bottom and he arrayed himself in it. I can't make Out how it was; but whenever anyone of us looked at that figure sitting in the prow arrayed in a silk hat and a sae-, we had to laugh, and he carried on about it, and chewed words over to himself so much, that we had to stop looking at him. At length we came abreast of Fiddlers Ferry; we could hear the barrel organs, and see the roundabouts, and the crowds of trippers who resort there on that Holy Day. And Gilbertson insisted upon lying at the bottom of the boat until we had drifted by, and the tones of the barrel organs didn't reach us any more, and we generously let him do it. In less than a quarter of a mile we were on another bank. We were debating lazily as to how to get off when suddenly someone shouted : " Look look! My God, we are lost !"

Every eye turned in the direction of the pointed fingure, and every heart in that boat quailea at the sight indicated. Sweeping round the bend with a dull cruel roar, came a huge mass of water, six feet high ; it was swerling down upon up like a terrible monster, with all the speed of an engine. And it was only 200 yards away ! We could see it dash over sand banks, and leave them covered with six feet of swirling eddying water; we could see it snatch 1, heavy logs of wood off the banks and dash them before it as tho' they were straws. Some one must have suggested it, for in a minute we had all leaped out of the boat into the shallow water; lifted the craft with its head to the oncoming rush; leaped back and waited; waited with a suspense which was shadowed by the gloom of an imminent danger. People had left their skittles on the green and were watching us. Old boatmen were shouting advice to us. But we could not hear it and, if we had, were too much paralysed to have heeded it. It seemed like an hour waiting those few moments watching that roaring speeding avalanche of water, tearing over the sand banks, like a great monster, to take us to its embrace. It is a few yards away! Now it's right over us! It's descending—every face is pallid, I close my eyes in apprehension—there is a still short dreadful moment, then a bang, a quick upward motion of the boat, and by the time my eyes are opened we are swirling round and round in the terrific current which follows one of these tidal waves. Probably impelled by fear we rowed in some sort of dangerous fashion out of the current which would have taken us home—as it was taking dozens of other boats—to what is known as the slack water. A width of about six feet of water at each side of a broad river always remains quite still while a current is running down the main channel. This is the slack water, and we got into it. There was a depth of four inches of water in the boat, everything was topsy-turvy,and the provisions were contently floating about. Slowly and dejectedly we pulled in the direction of Fiddlers Ferry. I lent Gilbertson my jumper coat to cover his shoulders. We reached the Ferry. Hundreds of people were thronging about there. We covered unluckly Gil- berteon over with a tarpaulin except those parts of him covered by the jumper jacket and the silk hat. Then we all went ashore but him. By and by all but McAllester returned and to our horroi found that Gilbertson had vanished—gone sure enough.. Suddenly we heard a shout and loud laughter, and looking, saw a sight enough to knock us down. Gilbertson was followed by a crowd. His silk hat was squashed on to his head; he clutched

at his sack trousers to hold them up with one hand, and had a jug in the other; his bony chest and neck were bare and be was trying to reach us by a series of jumps, the sack not allowing enough freedom to his legs to enable him to run. He was the most grotesque and silliest looking in- dividual I ever beheld. When he reached the bank he paused and cast one will despairing glance at us, and then jumped—jumped slush into all the black thick mud, and sprawled about like a crab in a bucket of treacle. The surging crowd of onlookers absolutely howled again when we dragged that animated mud bank into the boat. But the next moment there was another shout; and in a trice McAllester, followed by a shower of misiles, leaped into the boat with a cocoanut. In breathless confusion we pushed into the stream, and in five minutes McAllester, had broken his oar. We drifted down to the Ferry Bend ; the tide was turning; the currents had met and we found ourselves pitched about by three feet , waves.

Rowing was out of the question, and so we sat in momentary fear of being capsized until we were drifted to the bank. It was three in the afternoon ; and crowds of boats that had sailed gracefully by us during th 3 day were at home now; and we thought of the task we had. We found out now that Gilbertson had got into the predicament re- lated very easily. He sat patiently for some time when we had gone, but suddenly seeing the jug some distance away and no one about, he ' stole quietly out of the boat to get the vessel so as 'so do some "bailing out." But he no sooner reached the utensil than lie was spied, and the scene follow- ed. AlcAllester had spent a shilling at an Aunt Sally stall, and winning nothing he rashly concluded that he was entitled to a cocoanut and took one. But he had to run for the boat too. These things weren't treated as jokes, for everyone realized that they with other things had ruined the day's enjoyment. I'll not go into details as to how we got hack. It was a weary, dreadful journey. Gilbertson was made to put on his half-dried trousers and row. Poor fellow! Every stroke he pulled made him groan, and when asked what was the matter—some one was civil enough to do it once—he merely looked distracted, and said, "The-sand-oh-OTT Its the SAND." It was acting in his paws like emery paper would!

When we reached Bank Quay the day was done; no sounds save the gurglinir water fell upon our ears ; the stars and the moon were sailing silently above. We were cold and hungry and uncivil, and felt like so many boobies. Every two or three minutes there would be a break cm our moody, morose feelings by a row ; the boat that was so full of eternal friendship-swearing in the morning was now filled with personal recriminations and water. That trip was a failure. We had to pay £1 for damage done to the boat and for the lost oar ; and those who had borrowed "flannels" had to replace them with new ones. We had covered ten miles of water in 13 hours. We never spoke to each other after settling for the boat; didn't even say "Good night."

RUGBY. WARRINGTON v. WIDNES.

There was the usual large gate at Wilderspool on Saturday, to witness the return engagement between the above clnbs. The first match of the season, played a fortnight ago, ended in the unexpected victory of the Widnesians, and there was reason for that air of confi- dence which was prevalent amongst the ckemicals' sup- porters in the latest venture. Splendid weather favoured the match, and the gate was estimated at con- siderably above 6,000, of which nearly 1,000 had travelled with the visitors. Widnes played exactly the same team which carried them to victory in the first match, but Warrington were without Nevins, Donohue, and Webster. The homesters defended the pavilion goal, and
