having an advantage over Massey's reply, play was at first inside the home quarters. A free kick to the visitors was responded to by Evans. and the half-way line was crossed, whilst the position of the homesters was still further improved by Jolley, Higharn and Berry. Aldford grandly relieved, but some effective passing by the backs was supplemented by Jolley tackling Drum- mond. and Widnes compelled to the defensive. Barber was hurt for a few minutes in trying to stop a danger- ous rush, and on the resumption Jolley twice broke through, but in both instances there was that old eager- ness apparent, and glorious chances of scoring literally thrown away. Massey, from an essay, dropped at goal, and a minor was sent up. Evans and Heesom showed up on the drop-out, and Widnes were pressed on their lines, but as a result of tall kicking between Aldford, Barber (who had now returned) amd Massey, a hasty ex- cursion was paid to the home goal. It was left to Evans to clear the danger, and a free kick falling to the homesters' credit, a huge punt was well followed up by Jolley and Berry, and Forster saved in daring style. A Spell of stiff scrimmages, in which a laggard could not be seen, now occupied at- tention, and the monotony was at length relieved when the Bates, fleesom, Evans and Massey, passed neatly, the last-named being pushed into touch a few yards from the line. Ashton and Hampson made efforts to break through, but it was no-go, and another free kick to the visitors afforded them a slight relief. The home- sters would not be denied, and at this point were hav- ing matters pretty much their own way, the Bates claiming almost a monoply of the sphere. James Bate had a splendid chance after Brennan had a kick charged, but he preferred to bluff the Widnes custodian, with the result that the oval was knocked into touch. Hemp- son was again prominent in a line-out,and from a mark Fair. Turner had a shot at goal from half-way, the oval going outside. James Bate again gave his side the upper hand, whilst some good feotwork by Berry, Frank Turner, and Ashton created endless trouble to the Wid- nes custodian, whilst Boscow foiled any attempt to clear. Another display by the Bates, Evans, and Hee- sum was nullified by Drummond and Wright, whilst Gordon and Johnson changed the venue to half-way. The Warringtonians, however, were eager in the attack. and following another pretty display by the home backs, Wright, in attempting to relieve, kicked but poorly,and James Bate rushing between Aldford and Brennan Scored finely. Heesom was entrusted with the place, and landed a fine goal. Following the re-start, Drummond had a rare chance, after eluding the Bates, but he preferred to pass, and Massey and John Bate dribbled away, placing the oval at the Chemical quarters. So far Boscow had had but little to do, but a good kick be Brennan was well responded to by the home back, and the Bates again dis- playing accurate passing, Heesom and Evans passed and repassed, and a claim for a major point was overruled. Jolley dribbled to within a yard of the posts but Fleesom failing at a pass, another chance was missed. Brennan almost let in the homesters a moment later, but despite this, the Widnes forwards stood their ground manfully, and as a result of their endeavours they again caused play to rule at centre field. Berry was now too eager, and paid the peealty, but Fair Turner and Ashton led the homesters back to their original positions. Nothing daunted, the Widnesians forced their way back on the home lines, and Boscow being held near the posts, it looked certain that the visitors would score. However, Berry, Hampson, and Bandies led the homesters out of danger, and the Bates passing well, the interval was reached with the score of a goal and two minors in Warrington's favour. On resuming, exchanges between Clarke, Ald- ford, and Massey were followed by Evans making his mark. This player's effort was succeeded by another free kick, a too close attention by Wright of James Bate being productive of the Widnes goal being placed in peril. The Widnes forwards dribbled well, and Boscow was eventually reached, but he cleared splendidly, only to see the visitors return to the attack with renewed energy. Nevertheless, the custodian stood the test well, and Ashton gained considerable ground with a couple of kicks from lines-out. Aldford punted loftily to Heesom, and this player mulling, the visitors' looked certain scorers, but Boscow and John Bate luckily cleared. Fair Turner next got clear, and a splendid run half the length of the field landed the game on the Wid- nes lines, and twice appeals were made for legitimate points, Jolley apparently getting in from a long throw- out. Pretty passing by the Bates, Evans, and Heesom saw Massey failing to take a pass, and Clarke, Aldford, and Forster rushed down the open until near half-way, when Eleesom fielded well and dodged through his oppo- nents until well floored by Drummond. Evans took up the running, and almost got in, but Brennan was especially safe. Widnes now took advantage of mulling by the home backs, and for a lengthy period attacked. Boscow repeatedly made his mark under diffinulties,but relief was brought about by James Bate and Evans, the latter kicking and tackling Brennan close to his lines. The Widnes forwards asserted themselves, but the finish- ing stages of the game were fought well within the Widnes quarters, and the eed of a well fought game was a victory for Warrington by a goal and three minors to nil.

RUNCORN v. SALFORD.

This competition, which took place on Saturday after- noon, on the Irwell-lane enclosure, attracted a large crowd of spectators. The weather was all that could be desired, it being of a summerlike description. The fol- lowing were the teams :—Runcorn : S. Houghton, full back ;E. F. Morris, J. H. Crompton (captain), and T. Warder, three-quarter backs ; S. Abrahams, and J. Faulkner, half-backs ; A. Taylor, 0. Hughes, J. Davies, H. Farmer, W. Faulkner, T. Jordan, J. Langley, P. Riley, and H. Williams, forwards. Salford :A. G. Paul full back ; F. Miles (captain) A. Barratt, and J. Smith, three-quarter backs ;A. Ditchfield, and E. T. Henry, half backs ; T. Kent, T. King, J. Tewson, G. Cartwright F. Knowles, A. Knowles, S. Flaslam, J. Brierley, and T. Craven, forwards. Touch-line judges: Messrs. Sherlock (Runcorn), and Walmsley (Salford). Referee : Mr. P. Lodge (Broughton). J. Faulkner kicked off. As a result of long kicking between the opposing back players, the home team were forced hack to their 25 yards flag, and Miles sprinted across the ground but was unable to find an opening. Attempts at passing between the visi- tors were frustrated by good tackling on the part of Runcorn, and Houghtsm was cheered for some fine full- back play. Neat combined play between Smith, Bar- ratt, and Miles ended in the latter player drop- ping a goal for Salford. The kick off was quickly returned, and another attempt at goal by one of the visitors resulted in the ball going dead. After some tight scrimmaging,Crompton dropped at goal but missed the mark. Paul dropped out, Morris making a good run in reply, and sharp play by the Runcorn for- wards carried the ball over the Salford line, Paul being compelled to touch down. The home team continued to have the best of the matters, but Paul defended well, whilst Miles and Barratt made good but ineffectual at- tempts to force their opponents back to the centre. They were, however, unsuccessful and a series of scrim- mages tack place on the Salford goal. line, the latter be- ing lucky in escaping with a touch-in-goal. Half time was then called, with Salford leading by a dropped goal to Runcorn nil. Paul re-started, and Davies was quickly prominent with a fast run, but he finished with a pa.. 4 forward. From the scrimmage Morris got away, and when tackled passed the ball, which enabled Davies to score Runcorn's first try. Warder made a good but ineffectual attempt at goal. Salford had another spell of pressing, but Houghton was very safe, and a series of long kicks betweea him and Paul gave Runcorn a decided advantage in the matter of position. Quick passing by the Runcorn backs ended in Morris gaining the soconc: try for the home team, Hughes failing at goal. Passing between Crompton, J. Faulkner, and Morris resulted in the latter scoring Runcorn's third try, but Warder's place kick was again a failure. With only a few minutes to play Crompton scored a fourth try, and Warder kicked a goal, Runcorn finally winningbY ono goal and three tries to Salford one droppi d goil. Thus the game ended in the defeat of Salford by eleven points to four. The best team undoubtedly won,the fast, open play of the home forwards proving too much for the Salford icon. ST. HELENS TRAMWAYS COMPANY. PROSPECTS OF EXTENSION. -

The half-yearly meeting of shareholders in the St. Helens and District Tramways Company was held on Wednesday at the Fleece Hotel, St. Helens. Councillor Joseph Massey presided. The directors' report which was taken as read, stated that a satis- factory service of cars had been maintained, by which the traffic receipts had been increased from £4,198 10s. 2d. t. £4,449 Bs. A dividend at the rate of five per cent, per annum was recommended to be paid on the preference shares and three per cent, per annum on the ordinary shares for the half-year. That would absorb the sum of £416 10s., carry- ing forward a balance of £693 17s. 6d. The Chairman said the balance-sheet showed a marked im- provement in traffic receipts and in their management and mode of working. Referring to the fact that the company had to pay £BOO per annum to the St. Helens Corporation for maintenance of tram roads in the borough, the Chairman said it was hoped the Corpora- tion would see its way to considerably reduce that amount. There had only been one claim for accident during eighteen months, and that (£1) was paid with- out going into litigation. There was a balance of £2.778 18s. 9d. due to the vendor, and a sum of £5OO for an engine. It was proposed to raise second mortgage debentures to cover this, and they would rank next to the first debentures. Their present debentures amounted to £15,000 and behind that they had a capital of preference shares of £6,535, and on or- dinary shares of £16,875. Altogether behind the deben- tures they had £23,400, and the directors considered that a first-class guarantee for the second debentures. They proposed the dividend of 3 per cent., thinking it would not be wise to put it higher at present.—The report was adopted.—Councillor Massey was re-elected a director ; and Messrs. Hammill and Marsh were appointed audi- tors.—A sum of £lOO was voted to the directors for ser- vices during the past twelve months.—The Chairman stated that Mr. A. P. Smith, of Manchester, had retired from the chairmanship, and Mr. Boothroyd, of South- port, who was a considerable shareholder and keen business man, had been elected to the directorate in his place.—ln reply to a vote of thanks to the Chairman, Mr. Massey said he hoped at the next meeting they would be able to pay 5 per cent. to the ordinary share- holders. The directors had under consideration the extension of the lines at Haydock and also to St. Helens Junction, but they could nothing until some capital had I been raised. The prospects of the company, however I were very good.

LANCOHIRE AND CHESHIRE COUNTY 'BOWLING ASSOCIATION.

The sixth annual general meeting of the County Bowling association took place at the Midland Hotel, Withington, on Saturday evening, Mr. J. J. Meakin (Eersal), president, in the chair. There was a large attendance of delegates from affiliated districts, includ- ing Newton-le-Willows, Warrington, Wigan. Hindsford, Stretford, Chorlton, and Broughton. In the absence of Mr. Morecroft (Liverpool), the treasurer's report was read by the secretary, Mr. J. Singleton (Nesv ton-le- Willows). The balance sheet for the past year showed a surplus of £6 lb. 6d. on the seasom, the balance owing to the treasurer being reduced from £8 Ba. sd. at the close of 1891 to £1 15s. 11d, at the end of last year. The Chairman was re-elected president,Mr. J. W. G. Coombes senior vice-president, and Mr. John Gee (Wigan) junior vice-president. Messrs. Morecroft and Singleton were re-chosen treasurer and secretary. Mr. Fairclough (Newton-le-Willoyrs) moved that in future all contests should take place on a Saturday as the day of the week most convenient. Mr. Taberner (Wigan) on the other hand alleged tha!:Saturday was as inconvenient to some members as convenient to others, and others having expressed a like view it was decided to leave the matter in the hands of the Council. The draw for the first series of Lancashire ties was arranged to take place at the first Council meeting on the 15th of April. A War- rington representative moved that the first draw for ties be not taken in districts as hitherto, but the matter was referred to the Council for settlement. Application was made for the reinstatement of certain amateurs who had taken partici the Blackpool tournament, but it was left to be dealt with by the Executive. The following names were given in as district representatives : Withington, Mr. R. Williamson ; Wigan. Mr. Brindle ; Newton, Mr. Fairclough ; Warrington, Mr. E. Taylor ; Hindsford, Mr. Marshall ; Broughton, Mr. W. Fielding ; Chorlton-cum-Hardy, Mr. G. Lumb.

SHIP CANAL.

FLOODING THE SALFORD DOCKS. Some interesting work is in progress at the Salford docks. Hitherto these docks have presented the appear- ance of simple oblong basins, shaped in granite, with walls rising to a clear height of over thirty feet. On Saturday began the process of filling in these empty spaces with river water. It was expected that by Wednesday morning the water would have a depth of twenty-four feet, which is within two feet of the ordi- nary water-line to be observed when the canal is com- pleted. The flooding to this level is merely to serve a temporary puipose, and when this is accomplished the water will be allowed to sink by some eight or ten feet. Meanwhile the docks will have the look which we shall grow familiar with at some future day. The picture, seen from Old 'Trafford or from the long wharf which has been built at Stretford, will be that of a great inland lake, and nearly all the masonry of the docks will be lost to sight. The immediate object which the engineers have in view is to float a couple of huge dredgers, which on Saturday lay high and dry on a timber platform— technically called a " grid "—in the docks. These vessels were in the first place constructed and put to- gether in the shipbuilding yard of Messrs. Fleming and Ferguson at Paisley. If they had been meant for the lower portion of the canal they would have put to sea— for, clumsy as they appear, they are capital sea-boats— and sailing up the Mersey would have entered the waterway by the locks at Eastham ; but having to do service at the Manchester end of the canal, it was ne- cessary to take them in pieces and send them overland from Paisley. For the past few months the work of riveting and painting has been in progress at the Salford docks. A hundred and more haritiners have been ringing the day through, altogether there has been noise and activity at the docks of a character to suggest a Clyde shipbuilding yard. The work being completed, the next task was to float the Irk and the Medlock —for so the dredgers are appropriately nained,—and that is the business which had been taken in hand. Between Mode Wheel and Salford the river rose steadily on Saturday afternoon at the rate of about four inches an hour. The Irk, which is the more complete of the two vessels, will pass through the locks at Mode Wheel and get to work between there and Barton. The dred- ger Medlock will remove the dam at Salford and accom- plish such other excavation as is required. It should be said that the dredgers have a complement of twelve steel barges. Each barge is constructed to carry twenty boxes with a capacity of four cubic yards each, into which the dredged material is received from the shoots of the dredger. When filled the barges are taken to an adjoining wharf. By means of a patent orane the steel boxes are there lifted out, and the ex- cavated stuff is tipped into waggons and run to spoil. This system has been in operation for some months at Ellesmere Port and Millbank. The barges are 106 feet long by 18 feet beam, with a draft when loaded of 4 feet 3m. They have been built by Messrs. Gilchrist and Co., of Liverpool. The dredgers which feed the barges ake capable of lifting 500 tons of ordinary material in the hour. Each vessel is 132 feet in length, 37 feet in breadth, and 10 feet in depth. The engines are 350 in- dicated horse-power. The system of dredging in the ap- proach channel to the Eastham locks is oi a different character to that described. Here the practice is to shoot the mataial lifted, whether it be semi-fluid silt or hard rock, into hopper barges. The barges are taken to sea beyond the Mersey Bar Lightship, and there they drop their leads. The Ship Canal Company have no less than ten dredges at work, the two of greatest capacity lifting 800 tons of ordinary material per hour. All are of the "bucket-ladder " type. The following catalogue of ad- ditional plant may not be without interest for ;ome readers :—Nine steam grabs ; two steam hoppers, each with a capacity of 600 tons, three of 350 tons, and two of 140 tons ; ten towing hoppers, varying from 185 to 400 tons ;18 steel barges, each with a capacity of 120 tons, and six of 60 tons ; 57 wood punts and barges ; 12 steam tugs ; 17 steam cranes ; 22 locomotives, and 550 side-tip waggons. -- In connection with Canal matters, it may be interest- ing to state that the various deviation lines recently passed into possession of the different railway com- panies. The Cheshire Lines Committee having used one of the lines for goods traffic for about a month, opened it on Sunday for passengers, altogether waiving their right to use it for six months for goods traffic only. The old Irlam station will not see any more trains, the last having passed through on Saturday night. A fine new station on the higher level has been opened. The action of the Cheshire Lines Committee will very considerably facilitate the work of the contractor and of the Company

A FLEET OF BARGES FOR THE CANAL. Last week there was successfully launched from the shipbuilding yard of the Manchester Ship Canal Co., at the Sprinch, Runcorn, the first of a fleet of flat-bottomed barges for use on the Ship Canal when that waterway is opened. The craft is 69 feet long, with a beam of 141 t. 2in. The ends are semi-circular, and the sides almost perpendicular, thus giving the barge great carrying capacity, and it is expected to take a cargo of a hundred tons. The stem and stern posts are fixed with fittings to enable a number of these barges to be coupled toge- ther in one rigid train,drawn by a steam tuv,and steered by the rudder on the end barge. The barge is con- structed of English oak with a wych elm bottom, the latter a very hard wood which will sink in water, and yet the craft when launched only had a draft of ten inches. When it is loaded, the boat will not draw more than four feet. The fleet will be utilized for discharging large ships, which find it convenient to stop in the lower portion of the Ship Canal,and expeditiously despatching their cargoes to their destination. By reason of the small depth of water they require, the barges will be able to traverse the Bridgewater Canal to Manchester and as far as Rochdale. The barges are being built from the designs of Mr. Frank Wiswall, engineer of the Bridgewater Canal, by the company's own men, under Mr. John Dykes, the foreman. , ADMIRAL RICHARDS ON THE PROGRESS OF THE CANAL.

The report for 1892 on the navigation of the Mersey, made by Admiral Sir G. H. Richards, the acting Conser- vator, to the Commissioners for the conservancy of the river has just been issbed. On the condition of the Manchester Ship Canal at the end of last year Admiral Richards writes as follows :—"Two large dredgers have been employed during the year in forming the channel to the Eastham hocks. This channel is at present being dredged to a depth o 16 feet below old dock sill. Other • dredgers have been employed in deepening the ap- proaches to the wharves which are being constructed for the Shropshire Union Company at Ellesmere Port. Dredging is also being carried on to enable jetties to be constructed near Ellesmere Port, alongside which the Manchester Shin Canal and Dry Docks Com- pany, Limited, are about to moor pontoons for the repair of vessels. None of the com- pleted embankments or other works in the estuary have sustained any injury from the gales of the past year. The slopes in the clay cutting near Pool Hall have been flattened, drained, and faced with rubble stone. Timber wharfing has been constructed on the canal near the river Weaver. A large amount of traffic has been carried on at this point, and from its advantageous position for loading Weaver salt it has been called Sattport. During the six months that port has been established, steamers and sailing vessels have discharged and loaded cargoes here from and to foreign ports and coastwise amounting to 103,957 tons. In addition to this trade, all the traffic for Ellesmere Port has passed up and down the Ship Canal, and also a large portion of the salt trade from the Weaver Navigation. The total amount of traffic carried over the opened portion of the canal (about eleven miles in length) during the past year, exclusive of dredging material, was 1,124,594 tons. The Ship Canal works between the river Weaver and the Old Quay basins above Runcorn Bridge have been pushed forward with vigour. The lock above Runcorn bridge is now completed. The leek opposite the Bridgewater locks is in a very forward state and the gates are being hung, while the large Weston Mersey Lock' (which will farm the approach from the Mersey to the Weaver docksi at Weston Point) is making good progress, and will, it is expected be com- pleted by the end of the year. The estuary embankment from the river Weaver to Runcorn is new cornpleted,and for the greater portion of its length the stone facing is finished. A large part of this section of the canal is fully excavated, and the remainder will shortly be completed. The underpinning of the Cheshire abutment of the Run- corn bridge to meet the requirements of the London and North-Western Railway Company is now in hand. The deep clay cutting between the Old Quay docks and Bandies Creek is completed. From this point to Man- chester the cuttings are finished, end in many cases filled with water. The railway deviations near Warrington , and Irlam are complete, and it is hoped that the whole I of the traffic will shortly be passed over these lines. This will enable the Ship Canal to be excavated through the existing lines. Out of a total length of canal of 35i miles only one mile of excavation now requires to be completed, mainly consisting of railway embankments. ' The late autumn floods brought down a large quantity of silt from the river diverSions and other points into the canal, which has necessitated a large additional amount of dredging. To meet this and taopbreoivnigdecofnogrtrfuuctuterde maintenance two large dredgers re on the canal at Manchester, and are expected to be com- pleted next month. All the locks and sluices on the ' canal between Warrington and Manchester are quite ' finished. The Manchester and Salford docks are also , completed."

A REMINDER.

Sir,—Would you allow nte to call the attention of our Town Council to the fact that one portion of the borough is much more favoured than another in having the streets watered every day. During the dry, dusty sea- son, the upoer portion 'of the Latchford ward only receives a very small sprinkling once a week that being friday. We pay rates the same as other parts of the borough and I think we ought to receive the same con- sideration.—Yours &c.,

A RATEPAYER.

THE NEW LIBERALISM.

Sir,—l see that in your report of the debate on the above subject which appeared in your last issue, I am represented to have said that the policy enunciated by Mr. A. H. Crosfleld in his paper was " a rational and comprehensive policy which, if Mr. Crosfleld offered it to the Warrington constituency when he came before them as a Parliamentary candidate." I " would support with" my "own vote." This is only true with very considerable reservations. My leading reservations, so far as I can now recall them, were that the Conserva- tives should cease to pursue a progressive social policy, that Home Rule should be out of the way and that Mr. Crosfield should avow himself an Imperalist. And I was encouraged to make the declaration by noticing that he frankly expressed his disapproval of the Local Option Bill, and tacitly admitted that he was not in favour of disestablishment. His whole address was characterised by an independence of judgment, and an absence of mere party bias, which equally surprised and pleased me,and was in marked contrast to the usual deliverances of the latter-day Liberals with whom I have no manner of sympathy, and to whose policy I shall continue to offer all the opposition in my power.—l am, Acc., ARTHUR BENNETT. March 27th, 1893.

===== 0002601_18930401_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
-- BALDWIN v. CLOUGH.

Sir,—As there seems to be a widespread misconception as to our tenants, arising out of an answer by our Rhyl agent given to Mr. Justice Cave' during tho trial of this action at the recent Chester Assizes, we should be glad if you will give us an opportunity, denied by the judge to our representative, of explaining. The facts are briefly these. Baldwin became a tenant of our house, the Wynnstay Arms, Rhyl, on February Ist, 1892. He paid us £lO a year rent and we paid rates and gas. He left on August Bth, 1392. Clough then became our tenant at the same rental, we paying rates only. Contrary to our knowledge, Clough signed an agreement to pay Baldwin .£lOO for goodwill. This was the sum sued for by Baldwin. During the hearing of the case our agent in answer to the judge said, " There is no such thing as goodwill recognised by our firm." This is so in most cases, because we never make any ' charge to an incoming tenant ourselves nor receive pay- ments from outgoing tenants, and we do not believe in a tenant bolstering up a trade in a few months and then disposing of the business to his successor from whom he asks a heavy sum for so-called good-will. This is done in the interests of the tenants themselves, and to avoid disputes such as arose in this case. In this way we do not "recognise" goodwill. We discourage an incoming tenant from paying it, except in exceptional cases. As for the suggestion that any sum passing between incoming and outgoing tenants finds its way into our pockets we indignantly repudiate it, and can only regret that our representative, flurried no doubt by finding himself in the witness box, should have given answers which can bear any such interpretation.—Yours faith- fully, GREENALL, WHITLEY, & CO., LTD., Thomas J. Down. Director and Secretary. Warrington, March 28th, 1893.

===== 0002601_18930401_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT IS SALVATION?

•• If a son shall ask bread of any of you that is a lather, will ye give him a stone 9" Sir,—This appears to be an age of disillusion, an age of more or less rude awakening to the unreliability of lately cherished beliefs. Now such an awakening is not always pleasant to those so awakened. It may be salutary,but it is often painful to be rudely disillusionised of our pet beliefs. Whatever of error our belief may contain we love it, and hug it,—for is it not ours part of our very selves ?—and hence our abhorrence of the man who shows us the sawdust stuffing of our idolized bant- ling,and thus awakens us from our somnolent credulity to rub our eyes and blink at the sun of destructive cri- ticism. And this is a process which all thinkers who are worth their salt must go through, be the result what it may. Some prefer to bug their idols of credulity, however badly these may have been mauled and dis- ngured by criticism and comparison with facts. Others, more happy, abandon their idols when shamed of the truth. Your columns and those of your contemporaries, local and general, afford good examples of a third species who try to mix these methods—to hold with the hare and run with hounds. They try to reason and to believe ; each defending his own sectarian ideas. The reasoning,being usually in a vicious circle, they land where they started. The Baptist as a Baptist, the Methodist as a Methodist, and the Anglican as an Anglican—they all show the persistence of inherited belief. Men are, religiously regarded, subject to the law of heredity. They con- tinue to grow, in size if not in intelligence, where their fathers planted them, in ordered rows like cabbages. Now no man has the right to deny others the privilege of remaining in the faith of his fathers—if that is all he wants. But when he becomes aggressive and proclaims the utter damnation of everybody who does not accept his narrow creed, one may then, I think, with justice attempt to skow the merits of the other side. My objection to all your correspondents who attempt these discussions is that they never tell us what it is they are squabbling about. I ask Mr. Dignum for the bread of accurate information on this point and he has given us the stone of " Indian Missions." Now, setting aside the well known fact that these missions, like the mission to the Jews, is an enormous waste of good money and good men with a return of something not worth having, I would ask what is the message these missionaries are thus carrying to people better educated.very often, than themselves ? The elements of salvation? These are words. Kindly explain them. Tell us what salvation consists in, and how it became needful? I should be much pleased if Mr. Dignum, or Mr. Esmond, or anyone else, instead of ranging over such wide fields and getting EO much abroad, would give us the materials by which to judge as to the utility of their several contentions. And this all turns upon the ques- tion—What is salvation ?—I am, Sze. ISOS.

THE GRAPPENHALL VESTRY MEETING. Sir,—The prevailing opinion appears to be that the Easter Vestry is the great parochial council at which the parish business is investigated, and future action de- cided upon. This undoubtedly was the purpose of its origin. But what do we find in our rural districts? A 1 vestry meeting in name only ;not a tithe of the parishion- ers know anything about the parish business, and few of them care either. It is to be feared that this state of i things has been produced to a great extent by those who 219 responsible for parochial management. On Monday at the Vestry', a question WaS asked by myself' bearing on the financial business of the meet- ing. The Assistant Overseer .rhen appointed 12 months 1 ago, was appointed at a salary of about 2,311 per ' annum. In each of the half yearly accounts signed by the Overseers and Auditor, the amount shown was £lO, or £3B for the year. On the accounts being read over, the Chairman, Mr. Bradford, sen., without allowing any time for discussiontor even asking if there , was an amendment,put it to the Vestry that the accounts 1, be passed, which was accordingly done. My question ,was this: How in the accounts just presented, signed •by Overseers and Auditor, the assistant overseer was shown as receiving £3B for the year, when actually his salary was only about £3O. Mr. Oreenall, of Lymm. Clerk to the Dareshury Sessions, said I was out of order, in asking the question then, whereupon I replied that if

6

I was out of order, it was the duty of the Chairman to intorm me. lam afraid, Sir, that questions, especially when they cannot be readily answered, are very unwei- come in such places. It would seem that for some considerable time, the Assistant Overseer of this parish has been charged with receiving about £lO more as salary than he actually had. Whether he has had the handling of the money I do not know. But out of the £3B which he is shown as receiv- ing for his services, he pays, or there is paid, £5 per annum to the parish molecatcher, £3 for the out of poc- ket expenses of the Guardian,and some other items which brings the amount up to about £lO. Ido not say that I object to those sums being paid. but I do object to the form in which they are paid. If the payments are legal —and surely the Overseer and Auditor would not sanc- tion them unless they were ?—then let the accounts show how the sums have been actually spent. That the item is misleading, whether intentional or unintentional, there can be no doubt, and I shall consider it my duty to direct the Auditor's attention to the matter. What seemed like a big joke was made by Mr. Kaye, who moved that the office of parish constable be resus- citated, and that Mr. Joseph Fairhurst be appointed at the salary of £1 per year. The gentleman did not say, but probably he thinks, that as a parish we are becom- ing loose in our morals, and need a little extra looking after. I should suggest that the parish stocks should be put in order, for that too is an old institution. And could not Mr. Kaye fix on someone who for a trifle would be willing to become beadle of the Parish Church ? If old institutions are to be revived because of their being old,then let us go the whole length .But it is surely not too much to expect something different in the latter part of the 19th century, and amongst persons whose education . should lead them to seek the elevation of the masses, one would expect progressive measures,and not a going back to the old and darker time.—Yours,

S. H. JENKINS.

===== 0002601_18930401_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PROGRESS OF CHRISTIAN MISSIONS IN

INDIA.

Sir,—ln the "Wesleyan missionary notices " for this month, there is a paper by the Rev. W. H. Findlay on phases of mission work in South India." It is so pro- foundly suggestive, and expresses so admirably the real situation in that great field of Christian missions, that I avail myself of some of its remarks here by way of in- troduction. " Quality "says the writer" is of infinitely more importance than numbers in the present position of Christianity in India. In confining attention to the rate of numerical increase, the church at home is spend- ing needless anxiety upon a secondary matter. There is no more occasion to be anxious about the numerical advance of Christianity in India, than about the spread- ing of light when dawn has appeared in the East. That Christianity is the coming religion in India, is obvious now, not only to the vision of faith, but to the eye of ordinary intelligence and foresight. What has been seen 1 before in the history of our religion will be seen again in India ; it will run more risk from a too rapid, than from a too slow-paced progress. When once class move- ments to Christianity set in, and signs of the coming of that day may even now be seen—then, while those who measure everything by figures rejoice over" glorious accessions,"the wisest friends of our religion will tremble. The powers of evil that dwelt in the old paganisms of the Roman Empire sought vengeance on the Christianity that conquered them, by infecting it with their super- stitions and errors ; and so well did they succeed that three-fourths of Christendom is still sick of the disease. The seeds of infection in Hinduism are more active, virulent, and contagious than those that lingered in the dying Paganism of Rome ; the population of India is greater and more massed, and welded than were the populations of that Empire ; and the transformation from heathen to Christian will be affected in India in less than half the time that separated Christ from Con- stantine. When the rush and whelm of mass accessions begins, what is to save the Indian Church that is to be, from such grievous corruption as still pollutes the Greek and Roman communions 7" . . . . ..

_ . Now this passage whilst it seems almost to bend be- neath the weight of a most serious warning, conveys at the same time a very pungent rebuke to both hypochon- driacs and croakers. Mr. Findley refers to two import- ant agencies which may be employed to avert the disas- ter he particularly refers to in the passage quoted, namely the higher educational efforts now employed among the more thoughtful and influential classes, and the careful training and instruction of the present Christian churches in India. But I think we should not overlook the mighty agencies which have already been at work during a considerable part of the present cen- tury, namely the Bible, tracts, and other Christian literature which in hundreds of thousands have been poured broadcast from the native and foreign Press over the whole country. There was nothing parai.,l to this in the times Mr. Findlay relers to when the barbarous , tribes in Europe first embraced Christianity ;

no printed bibles, no tracts. no Ch ristiau literature deluging the lands, and acting as a foil to the superstitions and errors with which the powers of evil infected the earliest movements of Christianity in Europe Still there is great need for the most serious attention to Mr. Findlay's warning, and the means now in operation I conceive are a direct answer to it, and are at the same time an ample rebuke to those who are so ready to assert that our missions are making but little headway in India. For what are the facts ? In addition to the agencies NA named, there are the Protestant Mission Schools with over 299,000 pupils receiving an education in secular and Christian knowledge ; there are over 144,000 scholars in Sunday Schools devoted exclusively to bible instruction. There is the extraordinary work being carried on by the Zenana Mission among the women and girls of India. Some years ago this w.ork began among the English Baptists with two lady missionaries and half-a-dozen bible women, at two stations only ; now, in the report published last year of that one denomination, we read of " doors everywhere opened." and an earnest desire on the part of the woman in India to hear the gospel, of many who have been brought from darkness to light, of 22 stations, 54 lady missionaries, 160 bible women arid native teachers, with 64 girls schools numbering over 2,000 pupils. The women who are socially of a better class are immured in Zenanas, and it has been said that " the stronghold of Hinduism lies in these domestic prisons ; there the children receive their first impressions, there the ...ational life begins to take form ; it is there the mothers teach the first rudi- ments of idolatry to the rising generation, and weld its fetters on the infant limbs." Space will not permit me to refer to the various Educational Institutions of the higher class, such as the Missionary Colleges affiliated to to one or other of the Indian Universities. The Free

Church and the Established Church of Scotland are the leaders in this crusade against Brahmanism : with what success the higher education is telling upon this hoary superstition was instanced some years ago by a gentle- man returned from India, who stated that he put the question to a large audience of Hindus " how many of you educated Hindus believe in the Shastres ?" The answer of one man was "not one in a hundred," the answer of another who carried the assent of the meeting was, " not one in a thousand." (Mildraay Conference, p. 148). The total Christian population of India is not over two millions and a half. In 9 years, ending 1390, there had been an increase of 155,781 Protestant native christians or an average of 17,307 per annum. If we go back to 15 years ago, to the year 1877, the American Baptist, Sone Star Mission among the Telug,us affords us a most mar- vellous record ; for forty years their missionaries had laboured with scarcely anything to show for it, when in that year (1877) says the report " the pent up blessings of 40 years seemed to burst out at once into view, lilts a century plant, which absorbs sunshine and rain, and gives nothing back until the century is up, when it bursts forth in a beauty of foliage." In one day 2,222 were baptised ; in six weeks 6,730, and in ten months 10,000 ; the work had gone on to Mast, 1892, when a membership of between 30,000 and 40,000 was reported, the largest Christian Church it is said of any denomina- tion in the world." (The Baptist" Freeman "for May, 1892). Another great'American denomination—the Epil_ Methodists—seems to be'outstripping even the last named accessions. This mission which is labouring in the North-west provincies of India reports that " last year 19,000 converts were baptized, and so far from being over anxious for mere numerical accessions, it is stated that there were 40,000 others asking for admission into the church. Bishop Thoburn who has the oversight of that district declares that " the present outlook is the best since Carey's trumpet was sounded " So howful is he that' he will be surprised and disappointed if the ingathering of the next 8 years does not exceed that of the previous 92. These American followers of Wesley in British India maintain SO missionaries and 702 native ministers, the latter fact pointing without doubt to their marvellous success. Conversims have averaged about 800 monthly, and Bishop Thehurn efriec"s that the average monthly during 1893 will reach 2,000. Another eminent American missionary, Dr. Pentecost, affirms as the result of his observation "that the cleavage is going on everywhere in the rock of Hinduism—it is S'litting In all directions, and he believes that within the next 50 years the whole system will have collapsed." I might have greatly extended this review of .whit ho churches are doing in India—but suffidiept is here to 'stop the mouths or unfriendly critics. Apart from the evil wrought by the pernicious example of so-called cbristians—but who are nothing of the kind—the great enemies of missions in India are the sceptical agnostic, and atheistic systems imported from Europe and else- where. To these godless antagonisms, the speculative tendency of the Hindu mind, but tob readily attaches itself. But though the wheels of providence grind slowly there is an eternal power that makes for both truth and righteousness in this world of ours which will as assur- edly as to-morrow's sun rises subdue the opposition of all false science, false philosophy, and false religion, and establish the reign of Him whose authority shallow scoffers presume to set upon no higher level than that of Confucius or Buddha.—Yours, &c., JOHN DIGNUM. Penketh, 30th March, 1893.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, APRIL 1, 1893. NOTES ON GAMES A..A.) i TOWNSHIP MEETINGS. THE WARRINGTON TECHNICAL Al 1-1 FETICS. INSTITUTE.

The annual township meeting was held at the Red Lion Hotel, on Friday evening, Mr. Peter Gleave in the chair. Messrs. H. Middleton and P. Gleave were nomi- ated overseers, Mr. J. L.Jones, Guardian of the poor and waywarden, and Messrs. G. Forster, W. Dunabin, and G. Dodd, burleymen.

ORFORD.

The annual meeting of the ratepayers of Orford, con- tributing to the rates of the township of Warrington was held in the schools on Saturday evening. There were present Messrs. John Grounds, waywarden ; John Cann, Charles Grounds, J. L. Tunstall (collector), and E. Gibbon. The receipts for the year amounted to £345 las. Bd. ; the expenditure, £289 9s. 4d. ; and the balance in hand £5B 7s. 4d.—Mr. John Grounds was re-elected waywarden, and Mr. J. L. Tunstall collector.

The township meeting of the ratepayers of L'itchford was held at the Norton Arms on Monday morning, when there were present Mr. J. Lunt (in the chair), Councillors Shaw Green and Ashton, Messrs. Gillings, W. S. Haw- kins, Sampson, and Strange. The accounts which were read and adopted were as follows :—Receipts : balance against overseers, £515 43.1 d.; from rates £1,757 33.7 d.; other sources, £3l 14s. Bd. ; total £2,297 14s. Bd. Pay- merits :—Union Treasurer, £994 179.1 d. ; Rural Sanitary put horityTrea su rer,£l9 193 5d.; Borough Treasurer,£777 43. 4d. ; other payments, £ll3 83. 9d. ; balance againit overseers £39255. id. ; total, £2,297 14s. Bd. There was a balance of £430 to the credit of the overseers, but this was a reduction of close upon £lOO on the balance of last year. The rates levied during the year had been £1,750, and there had been expended £1,900. It was agreed that there was a likelihood of a still greater call being made upon the overseers during the coming year, and in face of this and the diminished balance, it was unanimously agreed that an extra rate of 2d. in the £ be lsvied for the first half year. This will bring the rate to is. 4d. in the £. Messrs. Lunt and Strange, the retir- ing overseers were re-elected for the ensuing year and thanked for their services during the past 12 months.

PENKETH.

The annual township meeting of Penketh was held in the Wesleyan schoolroom, last evening week, Mr. C. E. Parker, senior overseer, in the chair. The following gentlemen were recommended for appointment as over- seers :—Messrs. C. E. Parker, W. Ditchfield, and James Millington, and the names of Messrs. Aitkin, Boardman, Tetlow, E. Cross, S. Pusill, and Pownall were added in case of an emergency. Messrs. Tetlow and Seed were appointed burleymen. Mr. Millington was appointed waywarden. Votes of thanks were passed to Messrs. Routledge and Thorpe, the retiring overseers. The as- sistant overseer (Mr. J. Forster) submitted the accounts showing that the rates collected for the half year amounted to £378 9s. Bd., and the expenditure £378 The estimate for. the ensuing year was given at £4BO, the increase being due to the lighting and nightsoil con- tracts and the expenses entailed by the Rural Sanitary Authority in respect of the smallpox epidemic.—Mr. Millington explained the manner in which the question of widening the Widnes and Warrington-road, near Houghton's bridge, had been dealt with by the Highway Board.—lt was resolved that the overseers, waywarden. Messrs. Routledge and Garnett, and Dr. Murray meet Mr. Thompson (the surveyor of the Warrington District Highway Board) at the part of the road under consider- ation, for the purpose of conferring with him on the matter.

CELEBRATION OF THE MARRIAGE OF

MR. R,. E. GAItNETT.

On Saturday last the employes of the various departments of Messrs. R. Garnett and Sons. of Warrington and Penketh, were entertained to a knife and fork tea and social gathering in the Wesleyan schoolroom, Penketh, to celebrate the wedding of Mr. Robert E. Garnett, son of Mr. Robert Garnett, of Hall Nook. Provision was made for 200 persons. The tables and room had been tastefully decorated for the occasion. On the walls were to be seen various mottos : " Welcome," and " Long life and happiness to the bride and bride- groom." Amongst those who were present may be mentioned the Rev. John Gunnell, (formerly Superin tendent of the Warrington Wesleyan Circuit), Mr. and Mrs. R. Garnett, Mr. and Mrs. David Garnett, Mr. and Mrs. Robert E.Garnett, Mr.Riley,of Leeds, (father of the bride) ; Mrs. Elides. Manchester; Mrs. Coop, Southport; Mrs. W. Garnett, Chester; Mr. and Mrs. C. E. Parker, Mr. Dreg, Miss Fanny Garnett, Miss Hylda Garnett, and Miss Moses, Hall Nook ; and M. and W. Barlow. After a thoroughly enjoyable tea Mr. WILLIAM C. NicaoLsom on behalf of the employis wished the bride and bridegroom long life and much happiness. It did not appear long since he remembered the bridegroom as a little child, and thence passing through all the stages of early life, but to-day he was grown up and prepared to take his place in all the varied responsibilities of life. Every man as he came to such a period had received two educations. One given to him and one gained by himself. The latter was by far tiit racist important, because in that education he had to work out certain elements, and in the development of these principles he was ready to take up life's work. (Applause.) Mr. C. BONER= and Mr. CARTWRIGLIT baying spoken also on behalf of the employes, Mr. R. E. GAnNErr, who was received with applause, expressed his thanks for the great kindness shown to him that day. He should like to be allowed in the name of his wife and himself to thank one and All, both at War- rington and Penketh works for the unexpected presents made to them. He highly valued those gifts although so many other beautiful presents had been made to them from other friends. At the same time the expression of their goodwill was far more valuable than any other present that could have been made to them. He trusted that the good feeling which now existed between employers and employed would long continue He wanted everyone in the employ cf the firm to know and feel that he took a kindly interest in their welfare. (Applause.) Mr. RILEY, the father of the bride in a few remarks said that whilst sorry to lose the daughter of the house, yet he was pleased to know that her home and future amongst them would be to the mutual happiness of all. Mr. ROBERT GARNETT also delivered a short address, in which he expressed a hope that the kindly relations to which reference had been made, would increase. In the days in which they lived there was too little of the feeling which ought to exist between those who employed and those who were employed, but he believed he saw signs of better days. He looked hopefully to the time when there should be a deeper, a stronger, and a closer union between what were called class and class. He endeavoured to feel as he went amongst his workmen that they 'were brethren. He did not,perhaps,at times manifest the most brotherly feeling, hut he tried to cultivate that good feeling, and to look upon all his people at the works as brethren, not as slaves, not exactly in the light of servants, but as persons who helped to keep the business together. Their interests were often said to be identical, and they were identical. Some tried to set one interest against another, and in some places they were opposed, but to the detri- ment of both. He expressed the pleasure it had afforded him to hear rl the settlement of the cotton strike. =Mr. Garnett concluded by observiog that if Sir John Lub- bock introduced a Bill into the House, for a bank holi- day once a month, he would support it, if he were a member of Parliament: (Applause.) The Rev. Joint Gl:einem. also spoke, and the speeches terminated. The remainder of the evening was spent in further rambles in the grounds of Mr. Garnett's resid- ence and in listening to vocal and instrumental music in the schoolroom. The proceedings being brought to a conclusion about half-past ten o'clock.

MR. BRENNER AND THE REFORM OF PROCEDURE.

When the house resumes after Easter,fdr. Brunner will ballot for a day in order to take the opinion of the house on a waste of time to which attention was at the opening of the session called in the columns of the " Daily News." He will propose the abolition of the practice of the Speaker asking, after leave has been given to bring in a bill. " Who is prepared to bring in this bill 2" ITo would also no away with the practice of members walking up to the table bringing in bills and reports, and he would give the Government the privi- lege of having their bills reads first time without de- bate,and printed without obtaining the special order of the house.

FOOTBALL.

That incorrigible young man Of ours, the shrewd, the generous, P.D., is up the proverbial gum stick again. Unheedful of the lesson which his " ma " taught him the other week, he went speculating with his wages again on Saturday. He said he had a "dead snip "on with Widnes. He was so sure they were going to win again that he went to " Norton " and waited for the Warrington men at whom he shouted this, attuned In some wise to Chevaliers " Knocked em, &c."—" Hew moi dewntcher think yer fly ; but oh moi, ain't yer going to ketch it—ain't yer gewing to croi."

And of course his luck—his bad luck—didn't forsake him, and he lost his wages, and had the unfortunate pleasure of doing all the " croi " himself.

It was considered that the " gate" which witnessed .he match was about the largest of the season, and it was whispered to me that the takings would not be much ,ess than £lOO. This should cheer the hearts of our senior club, after the unfortunate losses which must have been caused by the postponements on account of the recent epidemic.

But let us get to the play—Hamlet said " The play's the—" What? Oh,no. of course it wasn't that kind of play. Anyhow, the play at Wilderspoot on Saturday, constituted an excellent game. It was an exceptionally keen competition in every respect, and although War- rington won by a goal, they were unable to exhibit very much general superiority over the visitors. It was all hard fighting. Widnes came with the intention of em- phasizing the verdict of the first game of the season ; and Warrington were equally determined to reverse it. It was a frequent and truthful observation that every inch of the ground had to be fought for, when one team gained ascendancy over the other. One thing which marked the play of Warrington was the fine discreet pas- sing. It was the best I have seen them put into vogue for a considerable time. There was, however, just a little bit of selfish play observant° amongst one or two individuals ; but then, what is man without ambition— whether it be ambition to score a try or to write the stuff that is attributed to that played-out instrument of the dilapidated comic writer's wit—the spring poet.

A few of the Widnes spectators were anxious to let people know they were about, when they saw their team I4ad lost the day. There were at least 30 female enthu- siasts of the game came from Widnes, which is a note- worthy point. And severAt of these enthusiasts could be heard criticising, in a " knowing " way the play of the respective teams.

Reverting to the match however, it is worthy of men- tion that Jolley and Higham distinguished themselves considerably as backs. The two Bates paid a strict attention to business ; Evans was" simply luscious "as centre, and " Surefoot " (Boscow) came out in grand contrast with Brennan, although he was " collared ' twice.

The Junior League teams are still plodding on expect- ant of that cup—not the cup of happiness—but the silver cup—offered by Mr. P. Davies. The Parish Church tram seem to be in for that silver ornament though ; they are leading the list, and on Saturday they met Latchford and beat them by a goal to a try. Garrity and Daniels showed up well for the winners,and Morgan Hughes, and Deakin carried off the " buttered scones," on the Latchford side.

The results if the other competition—not, the least though last—the Lancashire Cup Competition are thus :

ASSOCIATION.

Everton met Wolverhampton at Manchester, on Saturday, in the final and were knocked into a proverb- ial cocked hat. This however, has caused much " humpi- ness " amongst various individuals who have all turned on a torrent of abuse upon a simple minded mislead prophet, who writes for a Liverpool contemporary. Last week we dismissed the possibility of Everton being "spanked "and went so far as to suggest arrangements for "brass bands " and " triumphal arches," and suit- able music for" Conquering heroes," being made for the welcoming of the Everton boys, wh) according to him "were not going to sneak home by back streets, but bravely through the main thoroughfares." Well, Ever- ton were modest and didn't want that fuss making about them. The common proverb that has been used 'ln the matter is : " Don't count your chickens before they are hatched."

I am glad to hear that the Warrington Association Club is ping to enter the .medal competition and the difficulty arising from their connection with the Union has been amicably overcsme. The gate money which will be taken at the competitions will be devoted to charities, so I am told.

A week to-day it will be a matter of deciding, what will be the consequence of Greek meeting Greek, for then the Warrington Association Cricket Club are going to try conclusions with the St. Elphins team in the medal competition.

CYCLING.

Beautiful weather, plenty of dust, an abundance of fun, some lubrication, and a good "feed "at the Stam- ford Arms, attended the run of the Warrington Cycling Club to Bowdon on Saturday.

Something also happened, too. One of the clubmen was riding quietly along when two Manchester wheelers sped past. In a moment he bent his head over the handles and put on full speed after them. But he wasn't riding very long. In four minutes his head tried to knock a hole in a load of straw which was being carted along the road. And when he lifted his machine out of the ditch and got his bruises put right, he went home. Let US bops ho learned a lesson. But where was the much talked of restraining influence all time ? Echo—Where !

The Warrington wheelers were trying to banish dull care, last Monday night, when they held their last " smoker" at the Patten Arms Rotel. A splenlid vocal programme was submitted and let us hope the good at- tendance was not, as has been clandestinely suggested, the consequence of the tobacco being " free to all." There were several delegates from other clubs present. Amongst whom was B. Baker, of the " Cat" Holmes Chapel. He is one of the riders who once took a little trip of 140 miles in 12 hours,and on a solid tyre machine. But I heard that his machine wasn't even tired when he finished. Did someone say chestnuts ?

By the way T. Edge of Manchester ought to have pre- sided, but he didn't arrive. A splendid substitute was found in Mr. Kiernan, who made a very humourous speech relative to the possibility of the cyclist of England being called upon as a' fleet "army in the time of an invasion. What a grand thing this will be to protect Us from the fell vengenance of " blather- skiting " L'lstsr.

LOCAL PLACES OF ENTERTAINMENT.

EQUAL TREATMENT ASKED FOR.

At Warrington Borough Court, on Thurday, Mr. Acton applied for a special license for a musical entertainment in the Empire Theatre, on Good Friday. He promised that the entertainment should be as refined as possible. Alderman Sutton said it was a question whether they were justified in granting this application. It was not a usual thing. Mr. Acton mentioned the Royal Court Theatre's concert, but the Chief Constable said that the Royal Court Theatre people had no legal right to have any concert on that day. Alderman Sutton expressed an opinion that as far as ordinary entertainments were concerned, they ought to he omitted on Good Friday as Sunday. Mr. Acton said the Empire Theatre only wished tube placed on the same footing as other places. The Royal Court Theatre was allowed to have concerts on a Sunday. Alderman Sutton thought it was very unwise and im- proper proceeding to have entertainments such as were carried on in theatres on Good Fridays, and the inaeis-. trates had decided not to grant the license. Mr. Acton eskpfl if the Empire wonld be on an equal footing with the Royal Court Theatre, to vrhiela Alder- man Sutton replied that this decision had nothing to do with that place_ If the authorities of the Royal Court Theatre tend themselves open to any proceedings they must bear the consequences.

EXHIBITION OF STUDENTS' WORK.

There was a private view of the art work of the students at the Technical Institute, on Thursday. The exhibition this year is held in two of the second storey rooms of the museum, an arrangement made to facilitate private work at the School of Art, but an arrangement also which we think scarcely allows the same effectiveness to the hanging of the work. The work as usual comprises executions of various kinds of grades much of which is to be sent to the , Science and Art Department as certificate work. ' The number of exhibits, however, seem to us to be considerably less than last year, in the department of painting. In the textile, light and shade, and geometrical drawing,and'cast designing departments there appears to be both an increase in work for certificates, and a noticeable advance in the quality of the work, and on every hand abound traces of higher attempts, concentrated study, and careful tuition. The exhibition is the first which bas taken place since the School of Art was transferred to the Corporation in May last, since which time there has been a great influx of elementary students into the school whose work cannot be expected to be ready for exhibition at present. Still, although there is a possible decrease in the exhibits there is an increase in the work done at the school.

. _ The most popular department for inspection is gener- ally that ot painting. The most conspicuous work amongst these hangings is an exceedingly clever copy of Herkomer's "Hard Thnes,"by Miss Chrimes. The filling in of the details of this have been carefully executed. whilst the peculiar expression of the weariness on the countenances of the characters, has been caught very ingeniously. From Miss Chrimes's brush there are seve- ral noteworthy productions. What appear ,to be her two most successful originals are MI paintings of female heads. The first one is a life-size head and shoulders, the view being from, the back,but the head being turned so as to present the whole of one side of the face. The model is a blonde, and Miss Chrimes has succeeded in infusing a skilful freedom in the treatment of her sub- ject, which is a decidiA pronouncement of her success as a portrait painter. Especially in the way in which she has suceeeded in limning the alabaster white of the lower part of the face with a light crimson colour of the cheek, has she displayed her skill. The other bead is I also an original of a smaller sire, being the fall face and I bust of a young girl. This has been much admired,as a faultlessly-executed picture ;: it is considered to be one of her best productions, There areseveral other exhibits from the same lady's brush, and there are, ton, a few black and white sketches-1n all her exhibits Miss Chrimes displays an advance upon even the excellent productions which were shown last year.. Next comes Miss Burgess with a great number of works of different grades. One a clever full face portrait from life, a good likeness of a well-known Warrington young lady. Miss Burgess also shows a painting of an orchard which took a prize in the Sketching Club exhibits. The same artist is also ex- hibiting four very excellent originals, the subjects of which are rural cottages, filled in with cleverly treated rustic details. Miss Kerry has some praiseworthy studies from life in monochrome, and some still life studies exe- cuted as certificate work, She also has a clever water . . . _

colour painting of the interior of Grappenhall church, and one of the exterior of the same edifice, besides some &Torts at bird life. Miss Cooper has been busy in execut- ing certificate work in the still life group, one of her productions is a painting of a mandoline, with prettily coloured ribbons, and it is an admirably finished piece of work. She had also a cleverly treated original of a vase with ears of oats of wheat, the execution of which displayed much freedom of arrangement. There are some pretty landscape exhibits from the brush of Miss Broadbent, who has also a somewhat sombre painting of a corner of the Parish Church. The same student has a study of equine life which is rather lacking in light and shade. There are two finely executed hangings of poko-work by Miss F. Garnett. Miss Kate Brundrett has several exhibits. comprising landscape views and still life studies. Amongst the productions of Miss Spink's is a painting of a dessert table, under the rays of a table lamp. This had been cleverly executed in every way with the exception of the arrangement of de- tails, among which there seemed to be rather unneces- sarily included a saltcellar. There is a capital black and white study of smews by the Rev. A. Moore. Some praiseworthy certificate work by Miss Plinstone, and some excellent studies of • still life by Miss Jeans, Miss Shaw Green, Miss- F. Birtles, and Miss M. Hatton. We are glad to see a large number of water colours from Mr. Oswald Garside. They embrace various subjects including local landscapes sand studies of heads from life. The execution of these shows con- siderable abilty and give promise of better things from the artist. Mr. Stanley Pierpoint, assistant master, also exhibits a number of sabjects, amongst which is one that has been accepted for certificate by the Art Depart- ment. Mr. Millard and Mt. .T. I. Williamson have also some excellent exhibits. Amongst the productions in cast there were exhibits from Messrs. Millard, Stanley, Pierpoint, 0. Garside, Dainteth, Miss Kerry, Miss Lees, and Miss Apnleton. Some of the exhibits of Mr.Garside and Mr. Pierpoint have had the honour of being exhi- bited at the National Competition. Mr. H. Woodier has also a number of works on view that have been honoured at the National Competition. There is certificate work in drawing from Messrs. J. Millard, Dainteth, A. High:4p, Garside, S. Pierpoint, and Eyre, Miss Appleton, Miss Plinstone, Miss M. Longdin, Miss Birtles, Miss Kerry, Miss Cooper. There is also a considerable amount of textile work, and historic work, from various students exhibited for certificates ; and textile designs that have been accepted at the National Competition executed by Mr. Dooley. The public view of the exhibition opens to-day and will be continued till next Thursday. The exhibition will be open from 10, a.m. to CP p.m. each day. Ad- mission free.

SHOCKING ASSAULTS AT WIDNES.

At Widnes Petty Sessions on Thursday, before Mr. 11. Gaskell, jun., and Lieut.-Colonel Gaskell, Margaret Boyle, a married woman, of Pleasant-st., was brought up on remand charged with unlaw- fully wounding a man named Thomas Dice. Dice and his wife called in the Derby Arms on Saturday afternoon, when the prisoner paid for a glass of beer for a young girl. Mrs. Dice remonstrated with her for giving a little girl beer, and the prisoner threw the contents of a glass in her face. Dice spoke to her about bet conduct, whereupon she threw the glass in his face cutting him severely.—Dr. Butchinson deposed that there were several.cuts one above theleft eye, which had bled profusely, being a serious wound.—The prisoner was committed to the Quarter Sessions for trial. . _

Patrick Sewall, of D-ock-st., was charged with unlaw- fully wounding his sister Ellen Newel by stabbing her. At dinner on Sunday the prisoner asked his sister to give a girl named Gordon some dinner. She replied that she had no food to spare; it took her all her time to find food for him, whereupon the prisoner took up a knife from the table and stabbed her in the arm. He after- wards kicked her.—Dr. O'Weeffe said the wound pene- trated to the bone, but it was Not very dangerous.—The prisoner was committed to Liverpool Quarter Sessions for trial.

Thomas Cruise, a young raan of Marsh-st., was charged with committing art indecent assault upon Ellen Wailing, an old woman 70, years of age. The persons lodged at 2, Marsh-st., and an Saturday night screams were heard issuing from one of the bedrooms. Mrs. l'alsh, the landtady, went there and found the door secured but she forced her way in, and found the pri- soner and the old woman there.—The prisoner was act- ing indecently towards her.—The prisoner was com- mitted to the Quarter Sessions for trial. The magistrates sat from 11 am. to 7.30 p.m., eight hours and a half. The three cases given above occupied the greater portion of this time.

ALLEGED MURDEROUS ASSAULT ON A MOTHER AT WIDNES.

On Wednesday afternoon,at Widnes Police Court. before Mr. Deacon, a lad named James Allender, 15 years of age, residing at 90, Dozk-street, was placed in the dock charged with having Unlawfully wounded his mother on Tuesday afternoon.

Superintendent: Keighlev said it appeared that pri- soner and and his mother had a few words together on Tuesday morning about prisoner not having looked for work and for wearing his brother's boots. His mother turned him out and fastened the back door, but he came into the house the front way, and commenced to throw pieces of brick, stones, and -a saucer at his mother, and inflicted very serious wounds. B.S. Strickland reported that from what the last witness told him he went to prisoner's home about one o'clock On Tuesday afternoon. and found Mrs. Allender sitting on a sofa. Her face and head were bleeding very much. Ile noticed there were cuts on her face and nose, which he bandaged and dressed, but as he considered the injured woman was in a dangerous condition he sent for a doctor. The Sergeant apprehended prisoner at nine o'clock the same evening, and charged him with wounding his mother by striking her with bricks and a saucer, when he replied" She went to lift up the poker to hit me with it before I threw the saucer at her." Dr. W. Hutchinson stated that he had examined the injured woman, and found four wounds on the face, one above the nose a very serious one and penetrating to the bone nearly an inch and a quarter in length. There was another wound the same length on the bridge of the nose; the other two were smaller wounds. He took several pieces of a saucer from two of the wounds. Bricks would be likely to cause the others, but considerable force must have been used. It would be a week before she could attend the court. Superintendent Xeighley applied for a remand for eight days, which was granted.

DEMOLITION OF A GLASS WORKS.

On Tuesday, Mr. T. Sutton sold by auction the bricks of the old glass bottle works, which belonged to the late Mr. Richard Cartwright, an eye-Mayor of Warrington,as it is intended to demolish the greater part of the buildings. The old brick cone, which is over 100 years old, and muA contain thousands of bricks, realised.£69. There is an old painting in the Warrington Museum which represents a river scene at Bank Quay in the last century, with green fields and gardens leading up from the river banks,and on the picture is a representation of the cone about to be demolished and also another which was taken down some years ago. There are to be some great alterations in Factory-yard after the works are pulled down. Factory-lane is to be widened to double its present width, and the road leading to it from Liverpool-road is also to be widened. It is rumoured that after the railway alterations have been completed Thynne-st. will be continued under the railway past Messrs. Robinson and Skinner's glass works to join the widened Factory-lane. In that ease, the present sub-way under the railway at Bonk Quay Station will be stopped.

WARRINGTON TOWN COUNCIL.

The monthly meeting of this Council will be held" on Tuesday evening next for the transaction of the usual business. The matters to be considered in- clude the following :

THE COMMITTEES' ESTIMATES. OF PROPIIRTF.

The General Purposes Committee passed the following resolution :—" That in the opinion of the Committee the widening of tle.e four principal streets-of the borough should be proceeded with." The same committee re- commended that the action of the Committee in pur- chasing at the sale last week for Mr. George Dakin, and various blocks of property for a total sum of £25,020 be approved. It also has resolved :—" That the special committee appointed to negotiate for the purchase of property consider and report upon the letting of the above premises."

NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the next GEN- ERAL QUARtER SESSIONS ,f the Peace for the COUNTY OF CHESTER will be opened at the COURT HOUSE, NETHER KNUr-FORD, in and for the said County, on MONDAY, the 3rd day of April next, at half- past Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon, but in consequence of that day being a Bank Holiday the business of the Court will be adjourned to the following day at the same time, when the Grand Jury will be empannelled. The hearing of the Appeals arising within the Hundreds of Bucklow, Macclesfield and Northwich, forming the Koutsford District, and the trials of all prisoners tom- mitted from such Hundreds, will take ph ce at such Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Knutaford District must be in attendance at, the Court House, Knutsford, on TUESDAY, the 4th day of April next, at Half-past Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. THE Court will be adjourned to the Castle of Chester, on THURSDAY, the 6th day of April next, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Hundreds of Bmxten, Eddiabury, Nantwich and Wirral, forming the Chester District, and the trials of all prisoners committed from the last-men- tioned Iltindreds will take place at such adjourned Ses- sions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Chester District must be in attendance at the Castle of Chester, at the opening of the Court on THUIISDAY, the 6th day of April next, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. REGINALD POTTS. Clerk of the Peace for the County of Chester. Chester,l3th March, 1893.
